Helios Towers · HTWS-GB · the new Memo conveyor's card of 28 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Helios Towers

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 42,769 words, set out under Richard's eleven ratings in the order of the eleven ratings page: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the eleven ratings page uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

B+725th of 30

Memo topic 1, Tailwinds in the operating environment: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external forces that drive revenue and EBITDA are as favourable as they have been since the IPO, and the forces that act on the equity (rates, sector de-rating, frontier risk, tax and repatriation) are unfavourable; the business and the share are therefore being pulled in opposite directions. The demand acceleration is real, broad and visible in customers' spending, but partly front-loaded, so 2026 is likely the peak year for additions and FY2027 the peak year for reported growth carried by them. Competition and disruption are contained; the binding external constraint is buyer power at renewal and the sovereign layer, both of which matter more for the tails than for the modal path. The Consumer should build a confident operating model and a cautious equity bridge.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0258C-0555C-0740C-0945C-3560C-3966
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

C+467th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: competitive intensity is tightening slowly and only at the edges (new rural builds), not in the core business of filling existing towers; the binding competitive force for Helios is buyer power of its few large customers at contract renewal, not rival tower companies.

  • Core position intact. Helios is the number one independent in seven of nine markets, with 60 percent of marketable towers and 63 percent of independent towers in Tanzania, 42 percent of all towers and 75 percent of independent towers in the DRC, and sole independent status in Madagascar and Malawi. Colocation on an existing, permitted, powered site cannot be contested by a rival that has no tower there.
  • What changed in the last six to nine months. (1) Vodacom and Orange obtained a licence (April 2026) for Esengo Towers in the DRC, a joint venture investing 179 USDm in 1,000 solar sites over four years (up to 2,000 base stations over six years) in underserved rural areas. (2) SBA builds build-to-suit towers in Tanzania. (3) Indus Towers has licences in Nigeria, Uganda and Zambia with Bharti Airtel as anchor, on a model that is profitable with one tenant; none is a Helios market today. (4) MTN agreed to buy IHS (about 6.2 USDbn enterprise value), returning Africa's largest independent to operator ownership.
  • Magnitude. Esengo's 250 sites a year compare with about 500 new Helios towers a year group-wide and with 2,781 Helios towers in the DRC; it removes part of the rural DRC build-to-suit pipeline (and the colocations that would later follow), not existing tenancies. Indus Towers matters only if it enters a Helios market or sets a lower price reference for Airtel, Helios's largest customer (27.9 percent of revenue).
  • Direction. Mildly negative and slowly strengthening; the new competitors are customers or customer-aligned, which is why the real risk sits at renewal, not in share loss.
C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

C+468th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: input costs and availability are broadly neutral for EBITDA dollars and a mild headwind for the EBITDA margin percentage and for customers' affordability; supplier concentration is low.

  • Construction. New towers cost a stable 100k to 150k USD each; a leaner design uses about half the steel and a third of the concrete; colocations cost about 10k USD per tenant.
  • Energy. Diesel and electricity pass through via quarterly (about half of contracts) or annual (the other half) power escalators linked to local fuel prices, with floors. With Brent at about 111 USD, power revenue rises at nil EBITDA (10 USDm of Q2 2026 revenue at flat EBITDA), diluting the margin percentage; local fuel moves take 6 to 12 months to pass through, so a sharp rise causes temporary dilution and a fall a temporary benefit. The research's own monitoring threshold is a margin below 55 percent.
  • Second-order effect. Higher power bills raise operators' cost of using towers; the 149 percent rise in unrecognised contract revenue to 46.8 USDm (customer disputes and liquidity) is the place this would show.
  • Supply. Over 1,200 suppliers, 75 percent of spend local; equipment from India and China with months of buffer stock and fuel; generator makers report tight capacity (Caterpillar's backlog 72.1 USDbn), a lead-time risk, not a cost one; battery prices falling 8 to 15 percent a year help the hybrid programme (56 percent of sites hybrid at end-2025).
  • Local costs. Ground rent and security inflation are only partly covered by CPI escalators; a slow risk.
C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

F3426th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: macro acts on Helios more than on the average company through frontier currencies and sovereign risk, but it bites hardest at the sector level, through the discount rate applied to long-duration tower cash flows, and below EBITDA at the company level; the operating line is unusually insulated.

  • Sector level (hardest now). The US 10-year yield is near 5.10 percent with a Federal Reserve rise priced for December; the portfolio's Fitness for Fighting digest sets the telecom-towers Sector at Underweight and Momentum at Underweight in a confirmed risk-off regime, with towers called "the pure long-duration bond proxy". Helios's multiple is exposed even though its earnings are not.
  • Industry level. Emerging-market and frontier risk appetite is fading: Ghana's reserves and currency, Senegal's rating, African public debt above 60 percent of GDP; Morgan Stanley and Jefferies both value Helios at an 11 percent WACC.
  • Company level. 71 percent of Adjusted EBITDA (69 percent of revenue) is in US dollars, the CFA franc or the Omani rial; EBITDA growth shows an R-squared below 0.05 against currencies and Brent since 2015; a 10 percent adverse move in local currencies costs under 14 USDm of EBITDA. But reported profit fell to 21.7 USDm in H1 2026 from 30.9 USDm on non-cash currency movements on intercompany loans, and currency cost 5 USDm of Q2 revenue.
  • Gap in the research. The Gulf conflict that began in February 2026 (GCC output forecast to shrink 4.3 percent in 2026) is not assessed for Oman, about 9 percent of revenue and about 10 percent of EBITDA (MENA margin 72 percent in 2024). The rial is pegged and Oman's main terminals lie outside the Strait of Hormuz, so I judge the direct effect small and possibly positive through high oil prices for the state-controlled customer Omantel, but the Consumer should treat it as unexamined.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–45

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: four other external dynamics are material; together they are a modest net negative for the valuation and neutral for the fundamentals.

  • The pre-IPO holders' exit. Newlight Partners cut from 8.7 to 4.9 percent; Quantum Strategic Partners fell below 10 percent (July 2026). Deutsche Bank attributes the 23 percent fall from the June 2026 high to this supply. The overhang is finite (Newlight's remaining 4.9 percent is about 50m shares) and shrinking.
  • Operators re-taking towers. MTN's purchase of IHS at about 6.2 USDbn enterprise value (roughly 6 times IHS's 2025 Adjusted EBITDA of about 1,012 USDm, my arithmetic) shows an operator valuing tower ownership at an emerging-market multiple. That anchors the private-market value of African towers near 6x, so the "private buyer pays more than the stock market" route that the sector digest offers for European towers does not exist for Helios.
  • Europe's tower read-across. Inwit's anchor tenants served termination notices and the Milan court dismissed Inwit's injunctions in July 2026; the lesson for Helios is that uncapped CPI indexation, which Helios also has, can turn into a customer revolt when price outruns customers' economics.
  • Customer liquidity. Unrecognised potential contract revenue rose 149 percent to 46.8 USDm in FY2025 (a key audit matter); the top four customers are over 90 percent of receivables with no collateral.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

D5015th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the meaningful new-entrant threat comes from entrants adjacent to the customers (operator joint ventures, operator re-integration and operator-controlled tower companies), which carry captive demand; standard entrants are deterred by barriers, and orthogonal entrants (satellites) are not a threat on this horizon.

  • Adjacent (medium threat, rising slowly): Esengo Towers (Vodacom and Orange, DRC, 1,000 solar sites over four years); MTN re-integrating IHS; Indus Towers anchored on Bharti Airtel (not yet in a Helios market); Axian's captive towers. Their threat is to the build-to-suit pipeline and to renewal leverage, not to existing tenancies.
  • Standard (low threat): small independents and SBA; deterred by build cost, permits, aviation clearances, power logistics, the need for ten-year anchors and fifteen years of footprint-building. Replicating a country grid is described as "prohibitively expensive and practically impossible".
  • Orthogonal (low threat on four years): satellite direct-to-cell (Starlink, AST SpaceMobile, Kuiper) is physically capacity-limited; Open RAN and active sharing still need hardware on towers. The named tail is a second-generation constellation with exclusive spectrum in semi-rural Africa.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

A702nd of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: suppliers are a support, not a risk: spending is diversified, mostly local and buffered, and the one tightness (generators) affects lead times rather than costs.

  • Over 500 USDm a year of spend across more than 1,200 suppliers, 75 percent local; passive equipment from India and China with months of buffer stock and fuel inventory, an own tanker fleet and forward fuel buying in remote areas.
  • Cummins and Caterpillar are financially strong; Caterpillar's backlog rose 9.4 USDbn in a quarter to 72.1 USDbn, so standby generators have long lead times.
  • Lithium battery prices fall 8 to 15 percent a year; 70 percent of hybrid installations use lithium.
  • Risks: fuel delivery failures (floods, strikes) could breach uptime commitments; an old expert claim of 20 to 30 percent EBITDA losses from diesel theft is not consistent with 99.99 percent uptime and current margins, and I give it little weight.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0178C-0366C-0438C-0878C-3274C-3740C-4378
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

B+725th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: end-market demand is changing structurally and, in 2026, accelerating cyclically on top of the structural trend; the structural part has multi-year visibility, the acceleration has about 12 to 18 months of visibility and is partly front-loaded, so the Consumer should model a 2026 peak in tenancy additions, a strong FY2027 revenue year carried by that peak, and a reversion toward about 2,000 to 2,500 additions a year from FY2028.

  • Structural layer (high confidence, decade-long visibility). Wireless carries about 75 percent of African data against 15 percent in Europe; fixed broadband is 1.8 lines per 100 people in the footprint; coverage is 35 percent in the DRC and 45 percent in Tanzania; smartphone penetration rises from about 50 percent to over 80 percent by 2030; data in the footprint is forecast to grow twelvefold by 2040. Management puts the organic opportunity at 72,000 further tenancies across its nine markets by 2040, roughly double the current 34,455.
  • Cyclical acceleration (the last six months against the prior 12 to 18). Organic tenancy additions were 1,406 in Q1 2026 and 1,105 in Q2 2026 (consensus 487), 2,511 in the half, against 2,433 for all of FY2023 and 2,538 for FY2025: the run-rate roughly doubled. The cause is visible in customers' own spending: Airtel Africa's cash capex was 389 USDm in its June 2026 quarter against 121 USDm a year earlier, with FY2027 guidance held at 1.1 USDbn; Vodacom targets capex intensity of 13.5 to 14.5 percent and prioritises Tanzania and the DRC; Orange's Middle East and Africa eCAPEX is tracking to about 1.6 EURbn in 2026; the top four African operators raised capex guidance by 33 percent.
  • The caveat that matters for the model. Airtel calls its spending "a deliberate frontloading strategy". FY2026 tenancy guidance of 3,500 to 4,000 implies only 989 to 1,489 additions in H2, 495 to 745 a quarter, against 1,255 a quarter in H1. Management told a September 2026 conference it expects tenancy growth of about 6 percent a year over the medium term (about 2,100 a year on today's base) and a renewed operator capex cycle over five years, focused on colocations.
  • My path for the Consumer to test. Q3 and Q4 2026 at 700 to 1,000 additions each (management habitually under-guides; the pipeline gives "solid volume visibility into fiscal year 2027"), FY2026 at about 3,900 to 4,500; FY2027 at about 2,800 to 3,500; FY2028 onward about 2,000 to 2,500. Because tenancies added in 2026 earn a full year in 2027, FY2027 revenue growth stays near 10 percent even if additions fall; the deceleration shows in FY2028 (consensus revenue growth 7.0 percent, then 4.9 percent on two brokers).
  • Skew. Near-term upside to additions; FY2028 downside if 2026 was a pull-forward. The contract base (5.9 USDbn contracted, 6.5 years average remaining, non-cancellable, 1 percent annual churn cap) means the downside is to growth, not to the revenue base.
C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

B605th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the main disruption force, satellite direct-to-cell, is being disproved for any horizon that matters to an 18-month to four-year model, which acts positively on the company through the multiple; it remains a recurring perception risk because the technology and its financing headlines keep advancing.

  • Evidence against substitution. Starlink direct-to-cell carried 0.0003 percent of T-Mobile US traffic; a low-orbit beam spreads over about 14 kilometres so capacity is shared across all users beneath it; handsets transmit at 0.2 to 0.25 watts over 500 to 550 kilometres (about 3,000 times the signal loss of a tower at 1 kilometre); indoor penetration fails. Management, with a technical adviser, forecasts terrestrial networks carrying 97 percent of mobile data through 2040; Morgan Stanley, Jefferies, Deutsche Bank and Bernstein all treat satellites as a complementary rural layer.
  • Evidence of complementarity. Airtel Africa signed a direct-to-cell agreement with SpaceX across its 14 markets for text and messaging in unconnected areas; Kenyan trials confirmed low-bandwidth use only. Coverage extension can raise device adoption and later terrestrial demand.
  • Second disruption, slower: active RAN sharing and Open RAN let operators share radio equipment; protected in part by capacity-tied lease clauses and amendment fees; a five-year rather than an 18-month issue.
  • Residual risk. Equity markets "penalised" tower valuations on satellite fears; with SpaceX's financing and spectrum moves in the news (Bernstein, October 2026: "from broadband threat to wireless question"), episodes will recur and hit the multiple, not the earnings.
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

F3626th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: political and regulatory change is a standing headwind that is slightly worsening at the margin; it bears on the company below EBITDA (tax, cash repatriation, cost of capital), not on revenue, and its distribution is a low-probability, high-impact tail rather than a gradual drag.

  • Tax. Unprovided assessments total about 104 to 106 USDm: DRC environmental tax 39.9 USDm (2013 to 2016; moved only on currency retranslation), DRC 26.9 to 27.2 USDm (2020 to 2022), Tanzania 8.8 USDm (2017 to 2021), Congo Brazzaville 22.3 USDm (2021 to 2022) plus 6.5 to 7.9 USDm (2020). Directors judge outflow improbable. DRC minimum taxes bite even in loss years, part of why the reported tax rate was 71 percent in FY2025.
  • New in the last six months. The Banque Centrale du Congo will ban physical foreign-currency cash transactions from 9 April 2027 to strengthen the franc. The DRC is Helios's largest market (about 308 USDm of FY2025 revenue) and its hedge is that the economy is "innately dollarised". A ban on physical cash probably touches retail rather than electronic corporate settlement, but it signals de-dollarisation intent; if it widened to forced franc settlement, the largest hard-currency pillar would weaken. This is a dated item for the Consumer's watch-list.
  • Macro-regulatory stress in other markets. Ghana's reserves fell from 14 to 12 USDbn (March to September 2026) and the cedi weakened from 10.50 to 11.85 per dollar; Coface downgraded Tanzania from B to C (June 2026); Senegal is Caa1 with a negative outlook pending an IMF programme; M23 fighting in the eastern DRC touches 1 to 2 percent of sites.
  • Offsets. Tanzania's central bank is supplying dollar liquidity; Moody's upgraded Helios to Ba3 (February 2026) and S&P and Fitch to BB-; ISO 37001 with zero non-conformities.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

B+785th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: yes, the key industry driver, operators' capital spending on 4G coverage and densification, is accelerating the company's demand now, and the acceleration is broad (three large operators), not a single customer's project.

  • Airtel Africa: 389 USDm cash capex in one quarter, more than 920 sites added in that quarter, FY2027 guidance 1.1 USDbn, data per customer +56 percent; raised capex guidance by 20 percent (about 150 USDm) earlier in 2026.
  • Vodacom: subscriber growth of 16 percent in Tanzania and 11 percent in the DRC; medium-term EBITDA and operating free cash flow targets raised to early-teens growth; capex focused on Tanzania and the DRC.
  • Orange: Middle East and Africa eCAPEX up 10.5 percent in 2025, about 1.6 EURbn in 2026; Orange upgraded group guidance on African performance.
  • Omantel: 5G rollout and 3G retirement drove 454 Oman tenancy additions in H1 2026; Vodafone Oman is densifying as a new entrant.
  • Translation into Helios: the DRC and Tanzania led the lease-up (tenancy ratios 2.83x and 2.73x); Morgan Stanley links the guidance raise directly to customers' spending.
  • Persistence: probable through FY2027 on held operator guidance; FY2028 less certain because the operators themselves describe front-loading.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

B+704th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: customer demand is strongly supportive of the thesis now, and its fragility lies in timing (front-loading), affordability (power and currency costs at the operators) and concentration, not in the structural need for towers.

  • Support: Airtel Africa capex 389 USDm in one quarter against 121 USDm a year earlier; Vodacom raising medium-term targets; Orange raising guidance on Africa; Omantel's 5G and Vodafone Oman's densification; order pipeline into FY2027.
  • Customer health: Airtel Africa had net leverage of 0.7x and 32 percent local-currency operating free cash flow growth in late 2025; blue-chip operators are 98.4 percent of the contracted backlog; about 70 percent of revenue is from investment-grade or sovereign-linked operators; Omantel is 51 percent state-owned.
  • Concentration: Airtel 27.9 percent of FY2025 revenue (+24 percent year on year), Vodacom/Vodafone 23.0 percent, Orange 11.1 percent, Axian 10.3 percent, Omantel 7 percent. Axian is also a competitor through its captive towers (748 in Tanzania at 1.04x, about 2,100 in Madagascar at 1.3x) and is the least financially robust of the five.
  • Fragility signals: "deliberate frontloading" (Airtel); unrecognised contract revenue up 149 percent; a 63 USDm working-capital outflow in Q1 2026 on payment timing; high diesel prices raising operators' cost of hosting.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

F4024th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: regulators and political actors both help (by enforcing the barriers to entry) and hurt (through tax, currency and repatriation); the harmful side is the fragile one, because it is discretionary and can change suddenly.

  • Help: licensing, zoning, environmental and aviation clearances slow entrants; Tanzania repealed the rule requiring telecom licence holders to list 25 percent locally; Tanzania's central bank supports dollar liquidity.
  • Hurt: about 104 to 106 USDm of unprovided tax assessments; DRC minimum taxes; the DRC's ban on physical foreign-currency cash from April 2027; convertibility and upstreaming risk (cash is swept monthly to Mauritius and converted to dollars); Ghana's reserve depletion; Senegal's IMF negotiation; administrative burden in Tanzania (World Bank).
  • Fragility: high in the tail. The base case should carry a higher-than-statutory tax rate and some cash trapped locally; the stress case a convertibility restriction in one of the two largest markets.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

C7411th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: the realistic addressable market is large relative to the company (roughly a doubling of tenancies by 2040), and it allows tenancy growth of about 6 to 8 percent a year over the mid-term, which with escalators supports revenue growth of about 8 to 10 percent and EBITDA growth of about 10 to 12 percent a year; it is large in tenancies but each tenancy is small in dollars.

  • Management's organic addressable market is 72,000 further tenancies across its nine markets by 2040; Jefferies projects the tenancy market growing to 142,000 by 2030 with Helios's share rising to 30 percent; matching developed-market density would need about a million more towers across the region; operators still own most towers.
  • Drivers: population growth (about 600 million more people by 2040 in management's framing), data use per smartphone rising from 5.3 to 12 GB a month by 2031 in Sub-Saharan Africa, coverage gaps (35 percent of the DRC's population).
  • Constraints: rural single-tenant sites may never host a second tenant; new markets average 1.58x against 2.53x in established markets; customers' capex is cyclical and front-loaded.
  • Modelling anchor: management's medium-term "6 percent tenancy growth a year" equals about 2,100 additions a year on today's base; each 500 tenancies is about 5 USDm of in-year and over 10 USDm of annualised EBITDA (about 20k USD per tenancy a year).
1c

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0666C-1064
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

D5718th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the company's own cycle is in an up-phase whose rate of change has already peaked in tenancy additions (Q1 2026) and will plateau in revenue growth over FY2026 and FY2027 before decelerating in FY2028; the turn in the second derivative is the thing the market will notice first.

  • Reported revenue growth: 7.3 percent (H1 2025), 8.4 percent (H2 2025), 11.5 percent (H1 2026, Q1 12.5 percent, Q2 11 percent). Adjusted EBITDA growth 14 percent in H1 2026 (Q1 14.5 percent, Q2 13.4 percent).
  • Tenancy additions: Q1 1,406, Q2 1,105, guided H2 at 495 to 745 a quarter. Group tenancy ratio 2.09x (Q1 2025), 2.22x (Q1 2026), 2.26x (H1 2026).
  • Consensus revenue growth 10.9 percent (FY2026), 10.4 percent (FY2027), 7.0 percent (FY2028), 4.9 percent (FY2029, two brokers); EBITDA 12.1, 12.5, 8.9 and 8.2 percent.
  • For the next six months, H2 2026 revenue growth of about 10 percent is implied by consensus (947 USDm FY2026 less 466.3 USDm H1, against H2 2025 of 435.8 USDm), so the reported growth rate holds; the sequential fall in additions is the visible turn.
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

B647th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the external picture is one of strong fundamental tailwinds at value-chain level and headwinds at sector and valuation level; for an operating and financial model the net is clearly positive, for the share price over the next six months it is mixed.

Taking each thematic driver in turn:

  • African mobile data and 4G densification (value chain): strong tailwind, accelerating, evidenced in three operators' capex. Strength high.
  • Outsourcing of towers to independents (industry): tailwind with a long runway (operators still own about 70 percent of towers in Africa and the Middle East against 31 percent in Europe), but the MTN/IHS re-integration and the Esengo joint venture show the counter-current. Moderate tailwind.
  • Listed towers as an asset class (sector): headwind: bond-proxy de-rating with yields near 5.1 percent, Inwit's dispute, Cellnex's leverage; the portfolio's Sector tilt is Underweight. Moderate to strong headwind to the multiple.
  • Frontier and emerging-market risk appetite (industry and geography): headwind, stable to fading. Moderate.
  • Disruption (satellites): a fading headwind. Mildly positive.
  • Energy and inputs: neutral to EBITDA dollars, mildly negative to margin percentage and customer affordability.
  • Regulation and tax: standing headwind below EBITDA, slightly worse (DRC cash-FX ban; Ghana; Senegal).

Overall: tailwinds at the value-chain level are strong and broad; headwinds act on the discount rate, the tax line and cash repatriation. For the Consumer: model revenue and EBITDA on the tailwind; put the headwinds into the tax rate, the finance charge, the cost of equity and the risk scenarios.

The internal story is a well-executed, cash-funded pivot from building the platform to harvesting it: guidance is raised habitually, distributions have started and are scaling, and operating standards keep improving under an unchanged and proven team. C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

C6111th of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: execution and operating standards are rising on a long-running programme, cost discipline is strong, and governance standards have loosened slightly in 2026; the net is clearly positive for the operating model.

  • Execution: build-to-suit time 167 to 102 days; colocation delivery from ten days to two and now "under 24 hours"; power uptime 99.96 percent (2022) to 99.99 percent (2025), downtime 1 minute 10 seconds a week; hybrid sites 29 to 56 percent of the portfolio in 2025; procurement digitalised end to end; 63 to 65 percent of staff trained in Lean Six Sigma.
  • Cost discipline: FY2025 revenue +7.8 percent against cost of sales +1.3 percent; incremental colocation flow-through about 80 percent; headcount 634 (+3.8 percent).
  • Returns: group ROIC 10.3 percent (2022), 12.9 percent (2024), 13.5 percent (2025), 14.4 percent (H1 2026).
  • Governance (the negative): removal of adjusted gross margin as a disclosed measure in FY2025 as power-driven margin dilution became the live question; a Q1 2026 restatement of segment EBITDA for corporate recharges; pay maxima raised; a proposal to drop the 5 percent discretionary share-plan cap; 50.1 USDm of unsecured, interest-free related-party receivables (down from 96.0 USDm), whose counterparties the research does not identify.

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

Group judgement

Risks

D3618th of 30

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The operating risk is low and contract-protected; the equity risk is meaningful, and it comes from three places: the sovereign layer of the DRC and Tanzania, a concentrated customer base approaching renewals with new tower options of its own, and a valuation multiple exposed to rates and frontier sentiment through about 1.8 USDbn of net debt. Over the next six months the most probable negatives are perception-driven (a sequential fall in additions, rates, sector read-across); over eighteen to thirty-six months the most important fundamental risk is renewal pricing. For the model: a base case of indexation-only pricing, a renewal-concession downside, a repatriation-stress tail, and a multiple range of 6.5x to 9.5x forward EBITDA.

C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

F3025th of 30

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: several sudden, high-magnitude, low-probability fundamental risks exist, concentrated in the sovereign layer of two markets and in five customers; none is likely in eighteen months, but together they make the joint probability of no event low, and two of them (repatriation and renewal repricing) would hit the case, not just the price.

Each with my plausibility over eighteen months and its magnitude:

  • Convertibility or repatriation restriction in the DRC or Tanzania (the DRC's April 2027 ban on physical foreign-currency cash is the nearest precursor): about 5 to 10 percent; high magnitude (distributions paused, the case's cash leg blocked, equity down 20 to 30 percent).
  • Renewal repricing in the DRC, Tanzania or Oman cohorts (2027 to 2028): a material concession about 25 to 35 percent likely over 36 months, less within 18; medium magnitude (for illustration, a 20 percent cut on a quarter of revenue is about 5 percent of revenue, about 9 percent of EBITDA and about 25 percent of FY2027 consensus net income).
  • Customer credit event or dispute at a top-five customer (Axian the most exposed; disputes already visible in 46.8 USDm of unrecognised revenue): about 10 percent; medium magnitude (5 to 10 percent of revenue at risk; receivables without collateral).
  • Customer-aligned tower entry into a Helios market (Indus Towers with Airtel, Esengo beyond the DRC): about 10 to 15 percent; medium magnitude on growth, small on the base.
  • Tax assessments crystallising (about 104 to 106 USDm open): about 10 to 15 percent for a material part; low-to-medium magnitude (one-off cash of 1.5 to 3.5 percent of market value).
  • Sovereign default or sharp devaluation in Ghana, Malawi, Tanzania or Senegal: about 15 to 20 percent for one of them; low magnitude on EBITDA (71 percent hard currency), high on reported earnings.
  • Gulf conflict spreading to Oman's operations, or wider conflict in the eastern DRC: low; low-to-medium magnitude.
  • Earnings-quality event (unrecognised revenue above the 55 USDm tripwire, a restatement): low; perception magnitude.
  • Satellite technology leap with exclusive African spectrum and refinancing shock: very low on this horizon (debt fixed to 2031, the 2027 maturity pre-funded).
D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

C559th of 30

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the differences between the forecasts and the last four to six years are generated by one operating driver, organic tenancy additions on a fixed cost and capital base, which is itself driven by three customers' capex, so the operating change is highly concentrated but contracted and therefore predictable; the differences in earnings per share and free cash flow are generated by four financial lines (finance charge, tax rate, currency on intercompany loans, share count) and one cash line (capex normalisation), each independent of operations and each with a different and lower predictability; the Consumer should treat the case as a one-driver EBITDA story and a four-driver EPS story.

The differences, measured (history from FactSet and the company; forecasts the model's, with my corrections where stated).

Line FY2019 to FY2025 (what happened) FY2026 to FY2030 (what is forecast) The driver of the difference Share of the difference it carries Predictability
Revenue growth 14.1 percent a year, about half acquired (Oman, Senegal, Madagascar, Malawi in 2021 to 2022) 7.8 percent a year, all organic; 11.0 and 10.0 percent in FY2026 and FY2027, then 6.8, 5.5, 5.6 Tenancy additions (4,100, 3,000, 2,400, 2,300, 2,300) on a 15,270-site grid; escalators about +2 points; mix and currency about -2 Volume about 70 to 80 percent of growth in FY2026 to FY2027, about 60 percent after High for the level (contracted), medium for the rate from FY2028

Free cash flow after all capex and leases Negative to FY2023; about +30 in FY2025 About -52, 83, 154, 199, 226 USDm (cash basis with my lease corrections) Discretionary capex 230 USDm in FY2026 falling to about 115 to 120 USDm from FY2028 Capex normalisation is about 110 USDm of the 280 USDm swing; EBITDA growth the rest Medium; capex follows demand with a one-year lag and the 150 USDm Senegal commitment of July 2026 shows the build does not stop in 2027

How concentrated the operating driver is beneath the surface. Tenancy additions are generated by five customers who are about 80 percent of revenue (Airtel Africa 28 percent, Vodacom 23, Orange 11, Axian 10, Omantel 7) and by three countries that are about 76 percent of EBITDA (Tanzania 36 percent, the DRC 30, Oman 10, Deutsche Bank's split). Airtel Africa's capex alone (389 USDm in the June 2026 quarter, 657 USDm in its first half, about 1.1 USDbn guided for the year to March 2027, about 4,200 sites added in a half) explains most of the 2026 surge. One operator's capital-allocation decision therefore moves the growth rate; a 1,000-tenancy swing is about 20 USDm of annualised EBITDA (3.4 percent of FY2027) and, after tax, about 9 percent of FY2027 net income. Against that concentration stand the contract terms that convert additions into a floor: once a tenancy is signed it is non-cancellable for ten years or more, so the concentration is in the growth rate, not in the base. This is the sense in which the operating case is concentrated and predictable at the same time: one driver, but a ratcheting one.

How diffuse the EPS drivers are. On my corrected figures FY2027 net income of about 142 USDm moves about ±13 USDm for ±1,000 tenancies, ±11 USDm for ±5 points of tax rate, ±6 USDm for ±10 USDm of finance cost, and -30 to -50 USDm for a mid-sized currency loss; no single driver dominates, and the three financial ones are uncorrelated with operations (a currency loss arrives in a devaluation, a tax swing with an audit or a deferred-asset movement, a finance swing with a refinancing). The EPS forecast is thus the product of a narrow operating cone and a wide financial cone; the latter widens the product.

Against the past six years. The historical differences were also concentrated, but in different drivers: acquisitions (381 USDm in 2021 to 2022) and debt (leverage to 5.1x) generated the growth, and the useful-life change generated the first profit. The forecast replaces three external, lumpy drivers with one internal, contracted one. That is a genuine improvement in the quality of the drivers even though their number is smaller.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

C+6610th of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the strategy changed a year ago (IMPACT 2030, November 2025) from acquisition-led expansion to organic lease-up plus shareholder returns; in the last six months the change has been confirmed, resourced and lengthened (a 2040 horizon), and it is well articulated; its targets are already too low for the current run-rate, which makes an upgrade a likely future event rather than a risk.

  • The choices. Large-scale M&A deprioritised; capital ranked as organic growth capex (over 500 USDm to 2030, gated at about 20 to 35 percent incremental ROIC), then distributions (at least 400 USDm through 2030), with about 400 USDm kept as flexible capital. Builds only on ten-year anchor contracts.
  • The targets. Tenancy ratio above 2.5x and over 42,000 tenancies by 2030; organic Adjusted EBITDA CAGR above 9 percent (2026 to 2030); cumulative recurring free cash flow above 1.3 USDbn; ROIC 15 to 20 percent by 2030; leverage corridor 2.5x to 3.5x.
  • Resourcing. Discretionary capex raised twice in 2026 (180 to 210, then 215 to 245 USDm); buyback raised to 175 USDm; dividend started.
  • Why the targets are stale. At 34,455 tenancies at June 2026 and probably 36,000 to 37,000 at end-2026, the 42,000 target needs only about 1,300 to 1,500 additions a year to 2030 against 2,500-plus run-rates; Jefferies models 1.5 USDbn of recurring free cash flow for 2026 to 2030 against the 1.3 USDbn target. Management told investors it would update progress rather than change targets after Q1.

C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

F5028th of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the company is deliberately not entering new geographic or product markets, which is sensible given that its last wave of entries still earns below its cost of capital; the cost is the absence of a growth option beyond the nine markets.

C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

F4224th of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: price rises are nominal and contractual, not real; they protect revenue in real terms on hard-currency contracts but do not raise it, and the latent direction of real pricing at renewal is downward, not upward.

  • Mechanisms: annual CPI escalators on all leases, uncapped, indexed to the transaction currency (US CPI on dollar revenue), applied between December and February; power escalators (half quarterly, half annual) with floors; amendment fees when tenants add equipment or wind-load; no discount for secondary tenants; contractual floors against negative inflation.
  • Size: CPI and power escalators contributed about 2 to 3 points of revenue growth; in FY2025 they added 43 USDm, offsetting a 28 USDm currency translation drag; the power component lifts revenue at nil EBITDA.
  • Real price: no evidence of increases above cost inflation; an expert (former regional sales manager) expects declines once initial capital is amortised; another expert puts renewal compression at 20 to 30 percent; a former IHS executive says dollar and CPI linkage are "red lines" tower companies have held. Management does not present pricing as a lever.
  • Lesson from Inwit: uncapped indexation in a low-ARPU market became the cause of anchor tenants' termination notices. Helios's customers are more profitable (Airtel Africa net leverage 0.7x, Vodacom raising targets) and its price is about 30 percent below self-build, so the risk is lower, but it is the same mechanism.

Downtime in acquired portfolios fell 90 percent.

  • Within six months (to about April 2027):
  • Q3 2026 trading update, 5 November 2026: tenancy additions against the implied 495 to 745 a quarter; I expect them above that range (about 700 to 1,000), a probable small positive; a sequential fall from Q2 is near-certain and could be read negatively.
  • Continued buyback execution under the 175 USDm programme (about 4 percent of shares still to buy).
  • Airtel Africa's Q3 FY2027 results (February 2027): its own guidance implies lower quarterly capex than June's 389 USDm; a test of the front-loading reading.
  • FY2026 results and first FY2027 guidance (March 2027): tenancy additions of 3,000 or more and EBITDA near 590 to 600 USDm would confirm the step-change; the first final dividend paid from operating cash proves repatriation.
  • Refinancing of the March 2027 convertible with the pre-arranged term loan (removes dilution, adds interest).
  • The DRC's ban on physical foreign-currency cash from 9 April 2027 (a risk event to watch).
  • Novelty moderate; foundations strong.
  • Months six to eighteen (to about April 2028):
  • FY2027 guidance raised, on the record.
  • A refresh of the IMPACT 2030 targets (the 42,000-tenancy target is already within reach by about 2029), probably at FY2026 or FY2027 results or an investor day: the most novel step.
  • Leverage toward 3.0x and a possible lower corridor or a new buyback beyond 175 USDm; dividend growth above 10 percent.
  • A possible further rating upgrade from Ba3 and BB-.
  • Renewal outcomes in the DRC, Tanzania and Oman (either direction).
  • Novelty moderate to high; foundations moderate (depend on capex normalising and renewals).
  • Months eighteen to thirty-six:
  • Leverage toward 2.5x, distributions scaling toward the over 800 USDm Deutsche Bank models to 2030, ROIC toward 15 percent and the tenancy ratio toward 2.5x.
  • Possible adjacencies (ground leases, fibre) if resourced.
  • Novelty low (extrapolation); foundations moderate.

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

B+744th of 30

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

B+744th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The industry structure is about as favourable as tower markets get (concentrated, rational, protected by permits, power logistics and non-cancellable indexed contracts), and Helios is the most advantaged player in its core markets; the fragility lies with the customers, whose concentration, joint ventures and re-integration options set the terms of the 2027 to 2028 renewals, and with sovereigns, not with rivals or technology. The most important new observation for later sets is that the customer side is acquiring tower options: Vodacom and Orange through Esengo, MTN through IHS, and Bharti Airtel, parent of Helios's largest customer, through Indus Towers' African entry. None touches existing tenancies, but together they cap the long-run pricing power of independent towers and argue for a renewal-concession sensitivity in the model.

3a

Value chain dynamics

6 answers
C-3274C-3372C-3472C-3740C-3858C-3966
C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?B+704th of 30full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

B+704th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the route to market is direct and helps the company; there are no intermediaries to capture margin, and the only "intermediary" dynamic, operator sharing consortia, is mixed rather than hostile.

  • About 98 percent of revenue is earned directly from operators under master lease agreements; over 10,000 contracted partners execute locally; supplier forums run in five of nine countries.
  • Passive sharing agreements between Vodacom and Airtel in Tanzania and the DRC tend to favour neutral hosts and faster colocation; joint build vehicles (Esengo) and active sharing (the Airtel-MTN alliance in Uganda) cut the other way.
  • Fragility is low; the channel risk is the same as the customer-concentration risk.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

A761st of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: existing competitors are behaving benignly and are mostly retrenching or focused elsewhere, which helps the company; Helios's position against them is robust in its core markets and fragile only where it is sub-scale (South Africa, Ghana).

  • IHS: retrenching and being bought by MTN; it "rarely overlaps directly" with Helios's core markets; its group tenancy ratio is 1.51x (1.47x in its remaining markets) against Helios's 2.26x.
  • American Tower: optimising, prioritising developed markets and data centres; Africa and Asia-Pacific organic billings growth about 8 to 11 percent; Ghana is its stronghold.
  • SBA: building in Tanzania and buying ground leases.
  • Local independents: TowerCo of Africa and Eastcastle in the DRC, Minara in Tanzania, Al Karama in Senegal, Oman Tower Company in Oman; all minority positions.
  • None is pricing aggressively; none can match Helios's density where it leads.
C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?F4024th of 30full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

B567th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: disruption forces act mainly on demand-side actors (operators) and on capital markets; the company's operating position is robust, its equity position fragile.

  • Demand (operators): front-loaded capex, active sharing, joint builds and re-integration are the forces in motion; net positive today, but this is where the operating fragility sits.
  • Competition: shifting from pure independents to operator-aligned players (Esengo, MTN/IHS, Indus/Bharti); fragility rising slowly.
  • Regulators and politicians: de-dollarisation signals (DRC), tax assessments, convertibility; medium fragility.
  • Suppliers: generator lead times tighter, battery costs falling; low fragility.
  • Satellite operators: partners now (Airtel-SpaceX); perception fragility only.
  • Capital markets: the discount rate and the sector's bond-proxy de-rating are the strongest force acting on the share; Helios fell 8.2 percent from 13 to 23 October 2026 while Cellnex rose 6.9 percent, trading with London-listed Africa names rather than European towers.

3b

Industry structure

10 answers
Judgement70C-2480C-2582C-2675C-2778C-2875C-2966C-3170C-3560C-3662
Group judgementGroup judgement: Industry and Value Chain (Layer 1)B+744th of 30full answer under 3 Value chain dynamics and industry structure
C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

B+854th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Helios is the number one independent tower company in seven of its nine markets and number three among independents across Africa; where it is not first (South Africa and Ghana) it is small and those markets are immaterial to the case.

  • In its markets: about 68 percent of marketable towers across the footprint when operator-retained towers are excluded; Tanzania (4,255 sites) 60 percent of marketable and 63 percent of independent towers; the DRC (2,781 towers) 42 percent of all towers and 75 percent of independent towers; sole independent in Madagascar and Malawi (61 percent of marketable sites in Malawi); 57 percent of independent towers in Oman alongside the state-backed Oman Tower Company; in Senegal the only other independent is Al Karama with about 450 sites.
  • Where it trails: Ghana, where American Tower has 46 percent of all towers and 64 percent of independent sites; South Africa, where Helios has 388 sites against Mast Services (37 percent), IHS (27 percent), American Tower (12 percent) and SBA (9 percent).
  • Across Africa: IHS (about 28,665 African towers, now being bought by MTN) and American Tower (27,889 across Africa and Asia-Pacific) are larger; Helios has 15,270 sites and 34,455 tenancies. The continental rank matters little, because tower economics are local: a tenant chooses a site, not a company.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

A853rd of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the market is highly concentrated in every Helios market; the top five hold far more than 30 percent and the share curve is steep, passing the five-times test comfortably within each country.

  • Within markets, one or two independents hold most independent sites: in the DRC Helios holds 75 percent of independent towers against TowerCo of Africa and Eastcastle (cited as 25 and 14 percent of independent sites in one report, 19 and 14 percent of market sites in another); in Tanzania Helios holds 63 percent of independent towers.
  • Counting operator-owned towers, the top five owners (Helios plus the two or three operators' captive estates and one other independent) hold close to all towers in each market; smaller players are marginal.
  • Across Africa, four multinationals (IHS, American Tower, Helios, SBA) dominate the independent segment, with distinct footprints chosen "to hold leading market shares in chosen countries". The independent segment's share of African towers is given as about 52 percent (up from 39.9 percent in 2019) in one report, while Morgan Stanley says operators still own about 70 percent of towers in Africa and the Middle East; the definitions differ (Africa only against Africa plus the Middle East) and both point to a long outsourcing runway.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

B+784th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: market shares have been stable for years, and new entrants have taken no meaningful share over the last three to five years; the one large structural shift has been ownership moving from operators to independents, which helped Helios and is now partly reversing at the edges.

  • Helios's in-market shares are not reported as moving; customer-side shares are also stable (the top three carriers held a combined 92 percent across the footprint from 2022 to 2025, and most markets have three or four sizeable operators).
  • New-entrant gains: Indus Towers is pre-rollout and outside Helios's markets; Esengo Towers was licensed in April 2026 and has built little; SBA's and Minara's Tanzanian builds and TowerCo of Africa and Eastcastle in the DRC are minority positions.
  • Ownership shift: independents grew from 39.9 percent of African towers in 2019 to about 52 percent in 2025; Helios's own footprint doubled in 2020 to 2022 through purchases from operators (Oman from Omantel, Senegal, Madagascar, Malawi). The counter-current is MTN's purchase of IHS and the Ooredoo, Zain and TASC consolidation of about 30,000 MENA towers.
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

B+783rd of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: share consolidates over time toward the incumbent with the densest, best-powered grid in each market, which is the most advantaged player as well as the largest; Helios is that player in its core markets, and the mechanism is mechanical rather than heroic.

  • Mechanism. An operator adding coverage or capacity chooses an existing, permitted, powered site where it needs one; the incumbent's density means it owns most such sites. Each added tenant raises the site's return (about 12 percent cash-on-cash with one tenant, about 25 percent with two, over 30 to 34 percent with three) and lowers the unit cost of uptime, which strengthens the incumbent's position for the next colocation. Tenancies and Adjusted EBITDA have moved with a correlation of 0.96.
  • The advantage that decides in off-grid markets is power management: 99.99 percent uptime on 16 to 17 hours of grid power a day (8 hours in the DRC), worth a great deal to prepaid operators whose customers churn on dropouts (1 percent of downtime costs regional operators about 175 USDm).
  • Evidence in the numbers: the DRC (2.83x) and Tanzania (2.73x), the oldest and densest markets, led the 2026 lease-up; Senegal (1.21x) and Madagascar (1.32x) lag, showing both the runway and the time the mechanism takes.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

A861st of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: competitors put margin and return on capital first, behave benignly and build only against anchor contracts; industry prices do not fall when volume falls, because leases are non-cancellable, indexed and priced per tenancy.

  • Build-to-suit is initiated only on non-cancellable ten-year anchor contracts; there is no evidence of any tower company taking losses for share; the multinationals keep distinct footprints.
  • Pricing structure: no secondary-tenant discounts; amendment fees for added equipment; uncapped CPI and power escalators; all-or-nothing renewal clauses that block selective decommissioning.
  • The one volume-first model in sight is Indus Towers', which builds towers designed to return positively with a single tenant and is "potentially undercutting local pricing"; it is not yet in a Helios market.
  • The price pressure that exists comes from customers, not competitors: experts describe 20 to 30 percent lease-rate compression at renewal once a tower's capital is amortised; MTN calls renegotiation "highly complex"; management says intense pricing discussions are confined to Nigeria, where Helios has no exposure.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

B687th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the tower companies' attitude has been stable over the last eighteen months to three years and none shows "Michael O'Leary" instability; the one actor with the motive and means to launch a price attack is a customer-aligned entrant, Indus Towers, whose controlling shareholder's African arm is Helios's largest customer.

  • Stability: the four multinationals have behaved consistently; IHS is retrenching (selling Latin America, Rwanda, Kuwait and now itself to MTN); American Tower is prioritising developed markets and data centres; SBA builds selectively.
  • The shark scenario to watch: outside the files, Bharti Airtel is the largest shareholder in Indus Towers (about half the company), and Airtel Africa, a Bharti subsidiary, is Helios's largest tenant at 27.9 percent of revenue (237.9 USDm in FY2025). Indus is licensed in Nigeria, Uganda and Zambia with Bharti Airtel as anchor. If Indus extended into Airtel markets where Helios operates (Tanzania, the DRC, Congo Brazzaville, Madagascar, Malawi), Airtel would gain a captive alternative for new builds and a credible lever at renewal. That is the most plausible route to a price attack on Helios's economics, and it would come through new-build volume and renewal terms, not through cuts to existing leases.
  • Would anyone attack if they sensed weakness? Tower companies, unlikely; operators, through renegotiation and joint ventures, possible if their own economics deteriorated (high diesel prices, currency stress).
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

A772nd of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the industry structure is strongly supportive (concentrated, rational, high barriers, contract-protected), and its fragility lies not in rivals but in the concentrated customer side and in customer-aligned vertical integration, which is rising slowly.

  • Support: dominant in-market shares; benign, margin-first competitors; barriers of 100k to 150k USD per tower plus permits, aviation and environmental clearances and power logistics; switching costs that make relocating active equipment prohibitive; lease rates about 30 percent below self-build.
  • Fragility: the top four customers are 72.3 percent of revenue (68.9 percent a year earlier) and the top five about 80 percent; over 90 percent of receivables sit with the top four with no collateral; Vodacom and Orange have formed a build joint venture; MTN is re-integrating IHS; Bharti's Indus Towers is entering Africa; renewal cycles in the DRC, Tanzania and Oman fall in 2027 to 2028.
  • For the model: the structure supports the base case's volumes and indexation; the fragility belongs in a renewal-concession sensitivity (for example, a mid-single-digit percent cut to lease rates on the renewing cohorts in exchange for longer terms) rather than in the base case.
C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?D5015th of 30full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

D5416th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: substitutes are a low-to-moderate and slowly rising threat; the most important substitute is the oldest one, operator self-ownership of towers, now reappearing as re-integration and joint building, while technology substitutes remain marginal.

  • Self-build costs about 30 percent more than leasing; but operators still own most towers in the region, so ownership remains a live choice, as MTN's IHS purchase shows.
  • Active sharing reduces the tenancies needed where adopted (Airtel-MTN in Uganda); Italy's TIM-Fastweb RAN sharing across about 15,500 sites is the developed-market precedent; Helios's capacity-tied clauses and amendment fees soften it.
  • Satellites act as rural extension and backhaul; small cells and fibre become the contested ground in cities (the infrastructure bottleneck is said to be shifting to fibre readiness), an adjacency Helios has not resourced.

Strong business SRCA and BTE; management; financial profile

Memo topic 3, Value chain, structure, model, financials, management: the Rater's score

4a

Business quality

B+684th of 308 answers
Judgement74C-4176C-4272C-4378C-4480C-4550C-4655C-4780
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

B+684th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Helios's competitive advantage is strong, demonstrable and self-reinforcing in its core markets: contracts and switching costs lock in the base, power management and density win the incremental tenant, and each tenant widens the gap mechanically. The weakness is that the advantage does not translate into offensive pricing power, and the contractual part of the moat comes up for renegotiation with a handful of large, increasingly optioned customers. For the model: volumes and margins can be projected with confidence from tenancy additions and the ratio; pricing should be modelled as indexation only, with a renewal-concession scenario as the main downside.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

B+744th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Helios holds five real advantages; the two strongest (the contract structure and power management) are strong and the density advantage is widening mechanically, but the contract moat is time-limited and its renewal is where the customers will test it.

  • Customer-related (contracts and switching costs): strong, stable. Master lease agreements of ten to fifteen years, non-cancellable, all-or-nothing at renewal (no selective decommissioning), a 1 percent annual churn cap, the remaining value payable on early cancellation, automatic renewals extending to 40 years or more; relocating active equipment is prohibitively costly. Contracted revenue 5.9 USDbn with 6.5 years average remaining (H1 2026), up from 5.3 USDbn in Q1.
  • Production-related (power management): strong, widening. 99.99 percent uptime on 16 to 17 hours of grid power a day; uptime up from 99.96 percent in 2022; hybrid sites from 29 to 56 percent in 2025; operators call it "economically prohibitive to replicate".
  • Scale and density: strong in-market, widening. Number one in seven of nine markets; each added tenant raises site returns and the incumbent's claim on the next colocation.
  • Regulatory and political: moderate, symmetric. Permits, zoning and clearances deter entrants but also slow Helios's own builds and expose it to tax authorities.
  • Culture and knowledge: moderate to strong. Local execution (94 percent local staff), fuel logistics and security in the DRC, Tanzania and Madagascar; experts cite "superior localised execution versus rigid international operators".
  • Where it could narrow: the contract moat becomes a negotiation at each renewal (DRC, Tanzania, Oman cohorts in 2027 to 2028), and customer-aligned tower options (Esengo, Indus/Bharti, MTN/IHS) increase customers' bargaining alternatives over time.
C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

B+665th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Helios is mid-sized continentally but large where it matters, in each of its markets; scale confers density for colocation, purchasing and stock-holding capacity, and a cost of capital good enough to fund about 500 new towers a year, but not continental economies of scale.

  • Size: 15,270 sites, 34,455 tenancies, revenue about 950 USDm expected in FY2026, 634 employees, over 10,000 contracted partners. IHS (about 1.6 USDbn of revenue from continuing operations) and American Tower are larger across Africa.
  • What scale confers: in-market density (the source of colocations); over 500 USDm a year of procurement across 1,200 suppliers with buffer stocks; the ability to issue 500 USDm of 2031 notes at 6.75 percent and hold Ba3 and BB- ratings; a holding-company team that can run nine markets at 99.99 percent uptime.
  • What it does not confer: cost advantages across borders (each market's towers serve only that market) or bargaining power over the largest customers, each of which is bigger than Helios.
C-43How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?C7411th of 30full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

A802nd of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: the company's surplus customer utility is high and its inferiority against the alternatives is disproved by customers' behaviour: they lease about 30 percent below self-build cost, get world-class uptime and fast delivery, and keep adding tenancies at record rates.

  • Price: lease rates about 30 percent below an operator's total cost of owning the site.
  • Quality: 99.99 percent power uptime against an industry norm of 95 to 98 percent in comparable African geographies; every 1 percent of downtime costs regional operators about 175 USDm because 90 percent of subscribers are prepaid and switch on a dropout.
  • Speed: colocations in two days or "under 24 hours" (from ten days), new towers in 102 days (from 167).
  • Revealed preference: 2,511 tenancy additions in H1 2026; Airtel's revenue with Helios up 24 percent in FY2025; Vodacom confirms its Tanzanian tower transfer to Helios works as more tenants join.
  • The caveat: the utility is highest for colocation on existing sites; for greenfield rural sites operators are testing their own joint builds (Esengo), so utility there is contested.
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

B556th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: the company has defensive pricing power, demonstrated by the application of uncapped inflation and power indexation even through hyperinflation and devaluations, but no demonstrated offensive pricing power (real price rises); the relative utility has not eroded, which is precisely why the next test, renewal pricing, matters.

  • Evidence of application: CPI and power escalators added 43 USDm of revenue in FY2025, offsetting 28 USDm of currency translation; hard-currency pegging clauses in Tanzania and Ghana; inverse adjustment to exchange rates in Madagascar and Malawi; heavy devaluations in Ghana and Malawi "exerted minimal group-level financial impact".
  • No real increases: no evidence of price rises above inflation; amendment fees and no secondary-tenant discounts are value capture, not price rises.
  • Renewal: experts expect 20 to 30 percent compression once initial capital is amortised; the company's defence is the all-or-nothing clause and the absence of alternatives. The Inwit and Cellnex precedents suggest the likely outcome of a negotiated renewal is a modest effective price cut traded for a much longer term (MasOrange's Cellnex renewal was NPV-neutral), not a 20 to 30 percent cut, but this is unproven for Helios.
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

C-3813th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Helios shares its scale economies with customers once, at the outset (the roughly 30 percent saving against self-build), and then keeps the density gains (no secondary-tenant discounts); it is a toll model, not a Costco model, which maximises margin expansion now and concentrates the customers' incentive to claw it back at renewal.

  • Messaging is about partnership and reliability, not about passing density savings on.
  • Implication: margin expansion of about a point a year is retained by Helios; at renewal the operators will argue for a share of the density gains, which is the economic logic behind the expert's compression estimate.
C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

A821st of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: mechanical, gravitational forces rather than heroic effort improve the company's position and its financial outputs each period: every colocation raises site returns, lowers unit costs, deepens switching costs and makes the next colocation more likely to land on a Helios tower.

  • Tenancy ratio 2.09x (Q1 2025) to 2.22x (Q1 2026) to 2.26x (H1 2026); about 0.15x a year at present.
  • Site economics: about 12 percent cash-on-cash with one tenant, about 25 percent with two, over 30 to 34 percent with three; colocations need about 10k USD and return over 100 percent; incremental flow-through about 80 percent.
  • Financial outputs follow: tenancies and Adjusted EBITDA correlate at 0.96; the EBITDA margin rose from 53.2 percent (FY2024) to 55.2 percent (FY2025); ROIC rose from 10.3 percent (2022) to 14.4 percent (H1 2026).
  • Limit: lease-up in newer markets runs at about 0.1x a year, and some rural sites never fill.
4b

Management quality

B666th of 3012 answers
Judgement70C-5566C-5670C-5776C-57b68C-5872C-5972C-6074C-6182C-6278C-6358C-6464
Group judgement

Company - Management

B666th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Management is a proven, delivery-focused operator team with an outstanding record against its own guidance, outlier operating standards and a coherent, cash-focused strategy; its weaker points are governance scaffolding, a cluster of 2026 alignment choices and a capital allocation history whose acquisition chapter has not yet earned its cost of capital. For the model this means: trust management's operating guidance as a floor; expect it to be raised; expect capex to follow demand; expect distributions to grow as promised; and do not rely on management to make the EPS line clean, because it does not guide or explain it.

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

C+6410th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: the ambition is large but measured, more ambitious than its European peers on organic growth and deliberately less ambitious than its own 2020 to 2022 self on expansion; it is a force for compounding the existing footprint, not for outcompeting through new markets or products.

  • Planning horizon lengthened in July 2026 from five years to fifteen (to 2040), with a 72,000-tenancy organic opportunity, roughly doubling the base; IMPACT 2030 targets over 42,000 tenancies, a tenancy ratio above 2.5x, EBITDA CAGR above 9 percent, ROIC of 15 to 20 percent and at least 400 USDm of distributions.
  • Tone: the chief executive described the platform as "firing on all cylinders" and a "proven value creation engine" (July 2026), a step up from the confident tone of March.
  • Against peers: European towers guide to about 5 percent EBITDA growth; Helios delivers 13 to 14 percent. The leadership research reads Greenwood as risk-averse on new markets, which may cost consolidation opportunities.
C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

C+667th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: the strategy is smart, right for this stage and dynamic in one important way (capex scales up or down with customer demand); it widens the moat incrementally rather than boldly and does not yet answer the customer-aligned tower threat.

  • Right priorities: organic lease-up first (highest returns), builds only on ten-year anchors, ROIC-gated discretionary capex, M&A deprioritised, distributions scaled as leverage falls.
  • Dynamic: when operators accelerated in 2026, capex guidance rose twice; if they slow, discretionary capex can fall to maintenance levels and cash rises; the leverage corridor (2.5x to 3.5x) leaves room for opportunism.
  • Moat widening: hybrid power, cycle times, density; incremental.
  • Gaps: no resourced adjacency (fibre, small cells, ground-lease buyouts) and no stated response to Esengo or to Bharti's Indus Towers; a partnership or hosting role in operator joint builds would be the smart answer and is not mooted.
C-57

How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture (an enabler, not an ossifier)?

C709th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: judged by actions, Greenwood is a strong operator chief executive and an enabler: he ran the capital raising, the operations and now the harvest phase, each with measurable delivery; his record also includes the 2020 to 2022 expansion that still earns below its cost of capital and a 2026 stake sale.

  • As CFO (2015 to 2020): Adjusted EBITDA 54 to 227 USDm, margin 30 to 55 percent, over 5 USDbn raised, the 2019 IPO.
  • As COO (2020 to 2022): portfolio doubled from about 7,000 to over 14,000 sites through acquisitions in Senegal, Madagascar, Malawi and Oman; uptime to 99.99 percent; colocation delivery from ten days to four.
  • As CEO (since April 2022): leverage 5.1x to 3.4x; tenancy ratio target of 2.2x reached a year early; free cash flow turned positive in FY2024; fourteen consecutive quarterly EBITDA beats; IMPACT 2030; refinancing to 2031; buyback and dividend; record tenancy additions.
  • Against: the acquisition wave he co-led produced new markets at about 8.5 percent ROIC and the 2022 leverage peak; he sold 24 percent of his stake in March 2026 while the board raised his pay maxima.

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

F4023rd of 30

  • For shareholders: a single share class; buybacks executed at an average of about 168p, below today's 205p, when the board judged the shares undervalued; LTIP on free cash flow per share and ROIC; clawbacks and double-trigger change-of-control terms; a 1x base-fee shareholding requirement for independent directors; ISO 37001 with zero non-conformities; clean Deloitte opinions; monthly compliance declarations from each operating company.

  • Why: the shareholder-nominee era only ended in July 2026; the board is still being refreshed. Evidence of prudence "where it was not needed" is limited.
