Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

C-4613th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The economic engine is strong and predictable at the EBITDA and recurring-cash level, with genuine operating leverage, a long high-return reinvestment runway and margins rising about a point a year; it is weak and unpredictable at the equity level, where financial leverage, an erratic tax rate, non-cash currency items and an accounting-assisted depreciation line turn it into a thin residual. For the Consumer's model the critical judgements are: EBITDA can be built from tenancies with confidence; free cash flow after growth capex is roughly nil in FY2026 and turns positive as capex normalises in FY2027 and FY2028; leverage falls through EBITDA, not repayment; and the tax rate and finance charge, not operations, will decide whether consensus EPS is met.

C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

C5612th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: brokers'-basis EPS growth is very high (about 174 percent in FY2026, 44 percent in FY2027, 31 percent in FY2028 on consensus) on revenue growth of only about 7 to 11 percent; the operating growth that underlies it (EBITDA 9 to 13 percent) is durable, but the EPS line is a leveraged residual whose forecasts have proved unreliable, and the Consumer should treat its level as low-confidence and its direction as reliable.

  • History on the brokers' basis: EPS -0.100 USD (FY2023), 0.030 (FY2024), 0.033 (FY2025); revenue growth 28.6 percent (FY2023, inflated by Oman), 9.8 percent and 7.8 percent.
  • Consensus: EPS 0.090, 0.130, 0.170, 0.200 USD (FY2026 to FY2029); revenue 947, 1,046, 1,119, 1,174 USDm; EBITDA 528, 594, 647, 699 USDm.

EBITDA forecasts, by contrast, have been met or beaten for fourteen consecutive quarters. The pattern is EBITDA beats and EPS misses.
  • Durability: EBITDA growth is contracted and indexed; EPS growth depends on the tax rate (71 percent reported in FY2025), currency movements in finance costs and refinancing terms.

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

Capex per incremental tenancy in the 2026 mix is therefore about 70k USD, not 10k.

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

D2219th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: the earnings base is not clean: the gap between Adjusted EBITDA and reported profit is very large, one accounting change created the first profit, the tax rate is erratic and high, and non-cash currency items swing reported results; none of this touches the cash engine, but all of it makes EPS a poor anchor for valuation and forecasting.

  • Gap: Adjusted EBITDA 471.1 USDm (FY2025) against statutory pre-tax profit 136.0 USDm and net income 39.2 USDm; finance costs 163.7 USDm (company) or 182 USDm net interest (FactSet, including leases).
  • Depreciation: the 2024 extension of tower lives from 15 to 30 years cut depreciation by 52.8 USDm a year and "directly enabled" the first positive net income (FY2024).
  • Tax leakage: reported rate 38.9 percent (FY2024) and 71.0 percent (FY2025); tax expense 96.6 USDm against cash tax 45.5 USDm in FY2025 (33.2 USDm in FY2024); minimum taxes in the loss-making DRC entity; consensus implies 39 percent (FY2026), 34 percent (FY2027), 31 percent (FY2028) after minorities. This is the single largest uncertainty in the EPS bridge: holding 40 percent instead of 31 percent in FY2028 cuts consensus net income by about 13 percent.
  • Currency: non-cash movements on intercompany loans cost 86 USDm in FY2023 and cut H1 2026 profit by about 30 percent; brokers add them back, which is why brokers'-basis EPS (0.090 USD for FY2026) is far above the reported run-rate (0.019 USD in H1 2026).
  • Revenue quality: unrecognised potential contract revenue 46.8 USDm (+149 percent; key audit matter); accrued revenue 18.2 USDm (from 7.0 USDm).
  • Other: minority interests (38.6 USDm on the balance sheet) take a share of profits; non-recurring deal costs small (3.4 USDm).
C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

D4219th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: financial leverage is material and comfortable for creditors but strongly amplifies earnings per share and equity value for shareholders; it is the reason a 10 to 12 percent EBITDA compounder produces 30 to 45 percent EPS growth and also the reason the mark-to-market downside is large.

  • Balance sheet: FactSet net debt including leases 1,828 USDm (H1 2026), 3.69x trailing EBITDA; company net leverage 3.4x; gross financial debt 1,940.6 USDm at FY2025; corridor 2.5x to 3.5x; Ba3 and BB-. Debt is largely fixed: 500 USDm of 6.75 percent notes to 2031; swaps on the 387.4 USDm term loan to 2028 and 2035; a 100 basis point rise costs only 2.7 USDm a year of profit. The March 2027 convertible is pre-funded by a 250 USDm undrawn term loan.

The two roughly offset; together with the remaining buyback (about 4 percent of shares), the share count is a modest source of EPS upside to consensus.

Pricing power and operating leverage (and others)

Memo topic 5, Pricing power, leverage, cost control, feedback loops: the Rater's score

5a

Pricing power

3 answers

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

B+523rd of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: price will contribute about 2 to 2.5 points a year of revenue growth through uncapped CPI escalators, with power escalators adding or subtracting revenue at nil EBITDA; price is therefore worth about a quarter of revenue growth in FY2026 and FY2027 but about 35 to 40 percent in FY2028 to FY2030, so the business becomes more dependent on price exactly when the 2027 to 2028 renewal cohorts test it; my modal renewal outcome is a term extension traded for a modest concession worth about 1 percent of group revenue, not the 20 to 30 percent cut experts cite.

The mechanics and their size. All leases carry annual CPI escalators, uncapped, indexed to the transaction currency (US CPI on dollar revenue; about 69 percent of revenue and 71 percent of EBITDA is in dollars or pegged currencies), applied between December and February; in FY2025 escalators added about 43 USDm (about 5 percent of revenue, CPI and power together) and offset 28 USDm of currency translation drag. Power escalators (half reset quarterly, half annually, on local fuel and electricity prices, with floors) move revenue and cost together. Amendments (13 percent of tenancies) and colocations are priced at parity; there is no secondary-tenant discount.

Contribution to revenue and margin, my base case.

FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030
Average tenancy growth (volume) about 11.7% 9.5% 7.2% 5.8% 5.4%
CPI escalators on lease revenue about +2.0 points +2.0 +1.8 +1.8 +1.8
Mix (amendments, newer markets, lower revenue per incremental tenancy) about -2.5 points -1.5 -1.2 -1.3 -1.3
Power escalators (nil EBITDA) about +1.0 point +0.3 -0.2 0 0
Currency translation about -1.2 points -0.3 -0.3 -0.3 -0.3
Modal renewal concession 0 0 -0.5 -0.5 0
Reported revenue growth 11.0% 10.0% 6.8% 5.5% 5.6%

Revenue per average tenancy has been roughly flat (about 27.9k USD in FY2024 and 27.8k USD in FY2025, my arithmetic), because escalators are offset by mix and currency; I assume that continues, which is conservative if US CPI stays near 3 percent. The CPI escalator drops to EBITDA at about 60 to 70 percent (some site costs, notably ground rent and security, also inflate), so price adds about 1.3 to 1.6 points a year to EBITDA growth and about +0.4 points a year to the margin. Power escalators dilute the margin percentage: at Brent near 111 USD they cost about 0.5 to 0.7 points of FY2026 margin at unchanged EBITDA dollars; a fall in oil would reverse this.

The renewal test: what happens in 2027 to 2028. The cohorts signed in the DRC, Tanzania and Oman fall due for renewal or extension in 2027 to 2028 (Tanzania, the DRC and Oman are about 70 percent of revenue). The evidence on outcomes:

  • Against large cuts: all-or-nothing renewal (no cherry-picking of sites), a 1 percent annual churn cap, remaining-value cancellation payments, lease rates about 30 percent below self-build, about 50k EUR per site to move active equipment, and a footprint that would take a decade to replicate; Helios denied facing the pricing pressure MTN put on IHS in 2024 (Deutsche Bank, August 2024); the former IHS executive's "red lines" on dollar and CPI linkage; and, crucially, customers whose revenue grows roughly as fast as their Helios bill (Airtel Africa constant-currency revenue +20.8 percent in H1 FY2027, against Helios's revenue from Airtel +24 percent in FY2025, which includes new tenancies Airtel chose to add; Vodacom headline EPS up to +27 percent).
  • For some give: every peer precedent of a renewal under pressure ended in a trade, not a stand-off: IHS and MTN Nigeria (August 2024) extended to 2032 in exchange for majority-naira terms with a 20 percent cap on naira CPI; Sarana Menara (Indonesia, September 2026) extended tenors to 12 years for lower prices after XL Axiata and Smartfren merged; Indus Towers has offered escalation freezes for several years at renewals; Cellnex's MasOrange deal was NPV-neutral with short-term discounts for densification commitments; Inwit is now discussing caps on indexation for densification commitments.
  • The Inwit lesson applies only partly. Inwit's tenants revolted because uncapped indexation (fees +8.1 percent in 2023) outran falling Italian ARPU. In Helios's dollarised and pegged markets customers' revenues are growing at double digits, so the mechanism is weak; it is live only in Ghana and Malawi, where devaluation raises the local-currency cost of dollar-linked rent (Malawi's regulator is also considering more licences after a tariff backlash).

My modal outcome, probability about 50 to 60 percent: renewals with 10-year extensions and a modest give (an escalator cap or a freeze of one to three years on parts of the cohorts), worth about 1 percent of group revenue by FY2029, which I include as -0.5 points in each of FY2028 and FY2029 (about -11 USDm of revenue and EBITDA by FY2029). Material concession (a 10 to 20 percent cut on a renewing cohort, about 3 to 5 percent of group revenue): about 25 to 30 percent over three years, less than 10 percent within 18 months. Clean renewal with no give: about 15 to 20 percent. The cone is therefore skewed down in FY2028 to FY2029 and almost symmetric over the next 18 months.

What new customer options change. Esengo (Vodacom and Orange, 1,000 solar rural sites in the DRC over four years, 179 USDm), Eastcastle's financed expansion from 1,072 to 1,800 DRC sites (32.8 USDm loan, August 2026), Indus Towers' Africa entry anchored on Bharti Airtel (Uganda and Zambia under way, Nigeria scheduled; not yet a Helios market), MTN's re-integration of IHS and Airtel-MTN active sharing (Uganda, Nigeria) all raise customers' bargaining alternatives. They act on new-build pricing and renewal leverage, not on existing leases, and their main effect is on volume in the rural DRC from FY2028 rather than on the price of the core grid.

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7572P-0170
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

A801st of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: fixed-cost operating leverage is genuine and high on the way up (about 80 percent flow-through on colocations) and largely contract-protected on the way down (non-cancellable leases, a 1 percent churn cap), so it creates a narrow range of EBITDA outcomes and a wide range of earnings outcomes; the volume of additions is the swing input.

  • Cost structure: site costs (ground rent, security, maintenance) and central costs are essentially fixed; power is variable but passed through at nil margin; FY2025 revenue +7.8 percent against cost of sales +1.3 percent.
  • Sensitivities the Consumer can use: 500 tenancies is about 5 USDm of in-year operating profit and over 10 USDm of annualised EBITDA (Deutsche Bank), so 1,000 extra tenancies is about 20 USDm a year, about 4 percent of FY2026 EBITDA and, after tax at about 35 percent, about 13 percent of consensus FY2026 net income. A 1 percent revenue change from CPI escalators (about 9.5 USDm, almost all margin) moves EBITDA by about 1.8 percent and net income by about 6 percent; the same change from power pass-through moves EBITDA by nothing.
  • Downside protection: revenue cannot fall much (contracted 5.9 USDbn; cancellation requires paying remaining value); a demand stall shows as slower growth, not decline.
  • Width: FY2027 EBITDA plausibly 575 to 615 USDm (about ±3.5 percent around consensus 594 USDm) on additions of 2,000 to 4,000; net income range much wider because of tax and finance charges.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

A683rd of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: fixed-cost operating leverage will keep lifting margins by about one point a year to about 60 percent by FY2030, but the growth it acts on peaks in H1 2027 and then roughly halves; so operating leverage shapes the next four years as "high and falling growth on a rising margin", in contrast with the last six years, when growth was bought with acquisitions and leverage and the margin was held down by power and new markets.

The cost structure, measured. In H1 2026 revenue was 466.3 USDm, cost of sales before depreciation 150.9 USDm (of which power 100.9 USDm, 21.7 percent of revenue, and non-power site costs 50.0 USDm, down from 50.9 USDm a year earlier), and overheads about 58 USDm (my arithmetic: revenue less cost of sales less Adjusted EBITDA of 257.0 USDm). On an annual basis that is about 200 USDm of power (passed through at nil margin on price), about 100 USDm of site costs (ground rent, security, maintenance; fixed per site, partly CPI-linked) and about 117 USDm of overheads (fixed, growing with inflation). Of the roughly 420 USDm of cash operating cost, about 215 USDm is genuinely fixed and about 200 USDm is power that moves with revenue. That is why the incremental margin on a tenancy is about 80 percent while the group margin is only 55 percent.

How it acts on the line items, year by year (my base case).

FY2025 FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030
Revenue growth 7.8% 11.0% 10.0% 6.8% 5.5% 5.6%
EBITDA growth 11.9% 12.7% 12.6% 8.7% 6.8% 7.2%
EBITDA margin 55.2% 56.0% 57.3% 58.3% 59.0% 59.9%
Incremental EBITDA margin 76% 64% 70% 73% 72% 76%
Operating profit (statutory basis) growth about 17% 18% 18% 11% 8% 9%
  • FY2026 incremental margin is the lowest (64 percent) for three reasons that are each temporary: power pass-through revenue at nil margin (about 10 USDm in Q2 alone with Brent near 111 USD), a build-heavy mix (about 500 new towers, each earning about 12 percent on capital with one tenant), and about 5 USDm of currency drag on Q2 revenue. FY2027 recovers to about 70 percent as the 2026 tenancies earn a full year on sites already costed.
  • The margin bridge from 55.2 to 59.9 percent (FY2025 to FY2030): lease-up on fixed site costs about +3.0 points; overheads growing at inflation while revenue grows 8 percent a year about +1.5 points; power efficiency retained by Helios (escalators pass through fuel price, not fuel volume, so diesel saved by hybrid sites is kept) about +0.7 points; new-build dilution, power pass-through and my modal renewal concession about -0.5 points.
  • Below EBITDA the leverage is stronger. Depreciation grows only about 4 to 5 percent a year after the 2024 extension of tower lives to 30 years, so operating profit grows about 18 percent in FY2026 and FY2027.

Against the last six years. FY2019 to FY2025: revenue grew from 388 to 854 USDm (14 percent a year), but about half came from acquisitions (2021 to 2022, Oman, Senegal, Madagascar, Malawi) and the FactSet EBITDA margin rose only from 44.4 to 54.3 percent because each acquired portfolio arrived at a low tenancy ratio and with power-heavy costs; FY2023's 28.6 percent growth and 50.8 percent margin are acquisition arithmetic. The next four years reverse the mix: no acquisitions, slower growth (revenue about 7.8 percent a year FY2025 to FY2030), but nearly all of it organic and landing on a cost base that is already in place. The quality of the margin rise is therefore higher than in the past, and its direction is more predictable.

The judgement that matters for set three: operating leverage narrows the cone of EBITDA but not of growth. Because the contracted base (5.9 USDbn, 6.5 years average remaining, 1 percent churn cap) cannot fall, the downside is to the growth rate, not the level: with zero tenancy additions revenue would still grow about 2 to 3 percent from escalators and EBITDA about 4 to 5 percent. Each 1,000 tenancies above or below my path moves annualised EBITDA by about 20 USDm (about 3.4 percent of FY2027) and net income by about 13 USDm (about 9 percent of FY2027). My FY2027 EBITDA cone is 575 to 620 USDm around 598 USDm (consensus 594), skewed slightly up in FY2027 and slightly down in FY2028 because of the pull-forward discussed in P-05.

Where I differ from the consensus shape. Consensus EBITDA margins (55.7, 56.8, 57.8, 59.6 percent) are close to mine; my FY2029 revenue growth (5.5 percent) is above the two-broker consensus (4.9 percent), which I read as a coverage artefact, and my FY2028 revenue growth (6.8 percent) is slightly below consensus (7.0 percent) because I include a small renewal concession.

5c

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-7655P-0260
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?D4219th of 30full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

A741st of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: financial leverage will amplify earnings per share strongly over FY2027 to FY2030 because the finance charge is fixed at about 165 to 172 USDm while operating profit grows by about 50 percent; the balance sheet will de-lever only through EBITDA growth, not through debt repayment, because free cash flow after the 2026 capex surge goes to buybacks; and the refinancing of the 2027 convertible matters for cash and the share count, not for the P&L.

The debt stack (files plus one public fact). About 1.81 USDbn of financial debt at H1 2026: 850 USDm of senior notes due 2029 (7.5 percent coupon, outside the files, from public filings), 500 USDm of 6.75 percent notes due 2031 issued in April 2026 (which replaced the 2028 term loan and cut the cost of debt by about 40 basis points), 180 USDm of 2.875 percent convertible bonds due March 2027 after the 120 USDm tender, and about 280 USDm of other, largely swapped, facilities; plus about 225 USDm of lease liabilities. A 250 USDm three-year term loan, undrawn, pre-funds the convertible. A 100 basis point rise in rates costs only about 2.7 USDm a year. Weighted average maturity is four years; the next large maturity is the 2029 notes.

The finance charge, built from components (my estimate). FY2026 net finance cost before currency movements about 162 USDm: cash interest on bonds and loans about 128 USDm, lease interest about 23 USDm, non-cash accretion on the convertible about 6.5 USDm and amortised fees about 4 USDm, less interest income on about 200 USDm of cash. Adding the 21.8 USDm non-cash currency loss on intercompany loans already booked in H1 2026, the FY2026 total is about 184 USDm. Thereafter about 169, 171, 168 and 167 USDm (FY2027 to FY2030): stable because net financial debt stays near 1.6 to 1.67 USDbn, with a small saving from FY2029 when the 7.5 percent 2029 notes are refinanced at a lower rate on the improved rating (Ba3, BB-). Consensus implies about 173, 184 and 182 USDm (FY2026 to FY2028, EBIT less pre-tax profit, three to four brokers).

I disagree with the first set on the convertible. The first set judged that refinancing the convertible at about 7 percent adds about 7 USDm a year of interest. That is right for cash but not for the P&L. Under IFRS the convertible's liability component (148 USDm of the 180 USDm, with 32 USDm in equity) is carried at amortised cost with an effective interest rate of about 6.8 percent (my arithmetic: a 2.875 percent coupon plus the accretion of 148 to 180 over about 5.75 years), so the P&L already bears about 12 USDm a year. Refinancing 180 USDm at about 7.5 percent on the term loan costs about 13.5 USDm: about +2 USDm a year in the P&L and about +8 USDm in cash. Consensus FY2027 finance (about 184 USDm, up from 173 USDm) appears to carry the cash view; this is part of why my FY2027 net income (152 USDm) is above consensus (144 USDm).

The amplification, quantified. Operating profit rises from about 293 USDm (FY2025, statutory) to about 531 USDm (FY2030, +81 percent) while the finance charge before currency effects rises about 3 percent; pre-tax profit rises from 136 USDm to about 365 USDm (2.7 times). EBITDA covers the finance charge about 3.3 times in FY2026 and about 4.5 times in FY2030. Net income grows about 83 percent in FY2027 and 21 percent in FY2028 on EBITDA growth of 12.6 and 8.7 percent: the gearing is about 6 times in FY2027 and 2.5 times in FY2028, falling as the interest burden shrinks relative to operating profit. The implication for the Consumer: EPS growth decelerates much faster than EBITDA growth (from +90 percent in FY2027 to about +20 percent a year by FY2029), so the "EPS compounding" narrative will itself mature in FY2028.

The balance sheet path (my base case).

  • Absolute net debt rises about 90 USDm in FY2026 (free cash flow of about -30 USDm after the capex surge, plus about 60 USDm of cash distributions) and stays roughly flat to FY2030 because free cash flow (102 to 243 USDm) is returned through dividends (25 USDm growing over 10 percent a year) and buybacks (99 USDm in FY2027 to finish the 175 USDm programme; then my assumption of 110, 150 and 180 USDm under new programmes as leverage falls through 3.0x). The corridor (2.5x to 3.5x) is respected throughout; the lower bound is reached in FY2030, which is why I judge buybacks, not debt repayment, will absorb the cash.
  • The 3.0x leverage "by end-FY2026" that a January 2025 broker note attributed to guidance will not be met; about 3.3x on the company's basis is likely. The market should not be surprised (management now talks of the 2.5x to 3.5x corridor), but it is a datable miss against an old marker.

The share count: the biggest EPS lever that consensus ignores. Consensus net income over consensus EPS implies about 1.105bn shares (FY2026), 1.108bn (FY2027), 1.076bn (FY2028) and 1.10bn (FY2029). The company had 1,031.8m voting shares on 30 October 2026 (from 1,045.2m in issue on 30 April) and about 99m dilutive potential shares at H1 2026: about 61.5m from the convertible (the 120 USDm tender removed 41m shares, implying a conversion price of about 2.93 USD, about 219p) and about 37m from share plans. At the March 2027 maturity the convertible's 61.5m shares disappear unless the share price is above about 2.93 USD (7 percent above today's 2.75 USD); the buyback retires about 50m shares by end-2027 at today's prices. My diluted average for FY2028 is about 1,001m against consensus's implied 1,076m: about 7 percent of EPS upside from the denominator alone, before any post-2027 buyback. The risk to this is conversion: if the shares trade above about 219p in March 2027, 61.5m shares are issued and 180 USDm of debt disappears; net effect about -4 percent on EPS, so the denominator upside shrinks but does not reverse.

Skew and cone. Financial leverage widens the EPS cone: on my FY2027 net income of 152 USDm, ±1,000 tenancies is ±9 percent, ±5 points of tax rate ±8 percent, ±10 USDm of finance cost ±4 percent, and a FY2023-sized currency loss on intercompany loans (86 USDm) -57 percent. The EBITDA cone is about ±3.5 percent; the EPS cone is about ±30 percent. For the balance sheet the skew is benign: net debt is fixed-rate to 2029 and 2031, and discretionary capex can fall by more than 100 USDm a year if demand slows.

5d

Cost-out scope NTM

2 answers
C-7755P-0355
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

A603rd of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: little of the operating cost base is cuttable without damaging the uptime that is the product; the large cuttable item is discretionary capex, which can fall by well over 100 USDm a year if growth slows, turning a growth slowdown into a cash-flow upswing.

  • Power (largest operating cost): passed through; efficiency gains (hybrid, lithium, grid connections; Project 100) reduce consumption, and Deutsche Bank credits power efficiency with about 100 basis points a year of organic EBITDA growth; reducible gradually.
  • Ground leases: fixed, indexed; reducible only by buying land (Cellnex spent 270 EURm for about 300 basis points of margin); not resourced at Helios.
  • Security and maintenance: fixed per site; cutting them risks theft, sabotage and downtime.
  • Central and staff costs: small (634 staff); Lean programmes already embedded.
  • Capex: discretionary capex of 215 to 245 USDm (FY2026) against total FY2025 additions of 179.5 USDm, of which maintenance 37.6 and upgrades 28.7 USDm; consensus already has total capex falling to about 160 to 170 USDm from FY2027.
P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

C+556th of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: little operating cost can be eliminated without damaging the uptime that is the product; the realistic efficiency is about 20 to 25 USDm of EBITDA by FY2030 from power efficiency and overhead leverage, which arrives gradually and is already in my margin path; the large, fast "cost" lever is discretionary capex, which falls by about 110 USDm a year from FY2028 and turns FY2026's negative free cash flow into about 170 USDm by FY2028.

Line by line, what can reach the P&L and when.

Power (diesel and grid) about 200 USDm Diesel volume through hybrid sites (56 percent of sites hybrid in 2025, from 29 percent), lithium batteries (prices falling 8 to 15 percent a year), grid connections; Project 100 (44 USDm of 100 USDm spent by 2025, 11 USDm in FY2025) About 4 to 5 USDm a year, cumulative about 15 to 20 USDm by FY2030 (Deutsche Bank credits power efficiency with about 100 basis points a year of organic EBITDA growth; escalators pass through fuel price, not volume, so Helios keeps volume savings, per Jefferies, March 2026) Gradual, steady Medium

Why so little. The operating cost base is small and has been squeezed already: FY2025 cost of sales grew 1.3 percent while revenue grew 7.8 percent, non-power site costs fell in H1 2026, and uptime is 99.99 percent. Cutting security or maintenance risks theft and downtime, which is what customers pay for (each 1 percent of downtime costs regional operators about 175 USDm). The peers confirm the limits: IHS's opex fell in 2024 only through currency translation, and its hard-currency costs proved fixed; Indus Towers spent about 5.3 INRbn on energy in one year to cut diesel use 11 percent, which depressed its free cash flow by about 70 percent in the short term.

The capex lever is the one that changes the forecasts. Total capex (including intangibles) of about 285 USDm in FY2026 (discretionary about 230 USDm at the upper half of guidance, because demand is strong, plus about 55 USDm of maintenance and corporate capex) falls to about 200 USDm in FY2027 (about 3,000 additions, including about 450 new towers at about 125k USD each, colocations at about 10k USD, and the completion of 2026 builds already in capital work in progress of 163 USDm) and to about 170 to 175 USDm a year from FY2028. Consensus has 278, 168 and 160 USDm; I am about 30 USDm above consensus in FY2027 because the build pipeline carries over and because the company raises capex with volume (twice in 2026). American Tower cut its emerging-market discretionary capex by about 60 percent from 2021 to 2025 and IHS held total capex to about 246 USDm in FY2025; both show the lever works, with a lag of one to two years.

A timing caution on FY2026 cash. FactSet's half-year cash flow shows only 62.8 USDm of capex in H1 2026 against a full-year plan of about 280 USDm, while creditor days stretched from 28 to 32 (FY2025) and to about 52 on a later measure. If the H1 figure is correct, H2 2026 cash capex is about 220 USDm and payables unwind; if payables stay stretched, FY2026 free cash flow could be about 30 to 40 USDm better than my -30 USDm and FY2027 correspondingly worse. Either way the two-year total is unchanged.

Net, for the line items. Efficiency adds about 0.6 to 1.0 points of the 4.7-point margin rise to FY2030; capex normalisation adds about 110 USDm a year to free cash flow from FY2028. Neither is a step change, and neither is a reason to model the margin above my path.

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4780P-0562
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?A821st of 30full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

B+602nd of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: two reinforcing loops (density and cash-return) and three balancing loops (front-loading, capex-funding and customer-cost) are all at work; over FY2027 the reinforcing loops dominate the line items (full-year carry of 2026's record additions, falling unit costs, rising buybacks), while over FY2028 to FY2029 the balancing loops set the growth rate (additions roughly halve from 2026's level) but not the level, which keeps compounding; 2026's record additions strengthen the conditions for future colocations and margins and weaken, modestly, the conditions for future pricing and new builds.

The loops, how each acts on the line items, and which dominates when.

Loop Type Mechanism Evidence now (dated) Effect on line items Dominant when
Density Reinforcing Each colocation raises site return (about 12 percent one tenant, 25 percent two, over 30 to 34 percent three), lowers cost per tenant, raises switching costs and makes the next colocation more likely to land on a Helios site Tenancy ratio 2.09x (Q1 2025) to 2.26x (H1 2026); the DRC 2.83x and Tanzania 2.73x led the 2026 lease-up; time to a second tenant cut from 5 to 2.5 years Margin +1 point a year; incremental margin 70 to 76 percent Throughout; it is the engine of my margin path
Cash-return Reinforcing Falling leverage releases buybacks and dividends, which raise per-share cash flow and ratings, which lower the cost of debt Leverage 5.1x (2022) to 3.4x; Moody's Ba3 (February 2026); 2031 notes 40 basis points cheaper; buyback raised to 175 USDm (September 2026); dividend started Share count -16 percent by FY2030 in my base; finance charge slightly lower from FY2029 FY2027 onward, once capex normalises
Front-loading Balancing Operators pull capex forward; additions then fall Airtel Africa: "deliberate frontloading", 389 USDm cash capex in its June 2026 quarter against FY2027 guidance of about 1.1 USDbn (its remaining quarters average about 237 USDm); H1 FY2027 capex 657 to 713 USDm; precedents: Indian telecom capex fell from 927 INRbn (FY2024) to 596 INRbn (FY2026), US 5G capex peaked in 2022 and normalised within about two years Additions about 4,100 (FY2026), 3,000 (FY2027), 2,400 (FY2028); revenue growth peaks at about 11 percent in H1 2027 and falls to about 7 percent in FY2028 FY2028 to FY2029
Capex-funding Balancing, then releasing Growth capex to meet demand delays free cash flow and deleveraging; when demand normalises the capex falls and cash surges Discretionary capex guidance raised twice (180 to 210, then 215 to 245 USDm); absolute net debt up from 1,774 to 1,828 USDm in H1 2026 Free cash flow -30 USDm (FY2026), then 102 and 171 USDm (FY2027, FY2028) Balancing in FY2026; turns into a cash release in FY2027 to FY2028
Customer-cost Balancing More tenancies plus uncapped CPI and power escalators raise operators' tower bills, pushing them to joint builds, active sharing, re-integration or captive tower companies, capping future pricing and new builds Esengo (April 2026), MTN/IHS (2026), Indus Towers anchored on Bharti Airtel, Airtel-MTN active sharing; but customers' revenues are growing at 15 to 20 percent or more in Helios's markets Rural DRC build-to-suit volume trimmed from FY2028; modest renewal concession (P-04) Slowly, FY2028 onward; weak in the next 18 months
Valuation Mixed Satellite headlines and rates depress the multiple regardless of results; a lower share price retires more shares per buyback dollar Towers called "the pure long-duration bond proxy"; US 10-year near 5.1 percent; Helios fell 8.2 percent from 13 to 23 October 2026 No effect on EBITDA; a lower price is partly self-correcting for EPS: 100 USDm buys about 36m shares at 2.75 USD against about 27m at 3.70 USD Share price, not forecasts

Do 2026's record additions strengthen or weaken the conditions for future additions and pricing?

  • They strengthen colocation economics. More tenants per site mean more operators physically embedded in Helios's grid, which raises switching costs and makes the grid the default for the next densification (Vodacom confirms its Tanzanian tower transfer works better as more tenants join). Each 2026 tenancy is contracted for ten years or more and earns a full year in FY2027: about 2,500 tenancies of carry, worth about 25 to 30 USDm of FY2027 EBITDA beyond FY2026.
  • They weaken the next two years' volume. A part of 2026 is borrowed from 2027 to 2028. Airtel's own guidance implies its quarterly capex falls by about 40 percent from the June quarter. I model a fall of about 27 percent in FY2027 additions and a further 20 percent in FY2028.
  • They modestly weaken future pricing. The total bill per operator rises with each tenancy and each escalator; the larger the bill, the stronger the operator's motive to build jointly (Esengo) or use active sharing at the margin, and the more it has to negotiate over at renewal. Because customers' revenue is growing at least as fast as their Helios bill, this loop is slow; it shows in rural new builds and in the renewal give, not in existing rents.

The net for the line items. FY2027: reinforcing loops dominate (revenue +10 percent, EBITDA +12.6 percent, net income +83 percent, free cash flow positive). FY2028 to FY2029: front-loading and customer-cost loops set revenue growth at 5.5 to 7 percent and EBITDA growth at 7 to 9 percent, while the density and cash-return loops keep margins rising and EPS growing about 20 percent a year. This shape (a peak in reported growth in H1 2027, a visible deceleration in H2 2027 and FY2028, rising cash and buybacks) is the main thing the Consumer should test for predictability, because the market will first see the deceleration in tenancy additions in the Q3 2026 trading update (5 November 2026) and then in revenue growth in H2 2027.

To what extent is informal/qualitative company commentary above or below formal guidance and sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: informal commentary is above formal guidance and roughly in line with consensus for FY2027 and FY2028, and above consensus for FY2029 and beyond; the reason is that management talks about the multi-year runway while it guides only the current year and a stale 2030 plan.

  • Informal: "firing on all cylinders"; a pipeline giving "solid volume visibility into fiscal year 2027"; at a September 2026 conference, tenancy growth of about 6 percent a year over the medium term, a tenancy ratio of 2.5x within three years and 3.0x in the long term, and a renewed operator capex cycle over five years focused on colocations; 72,000 tenancies of opportunity by 2040; terrestrial networks carrying 97 percent of data through 2040.
  • Against formal guidance: 2.5x "within three years" (by about 2029) is ahead of the 2030 formal target; 6 percent a year implies about 42,000 tenancies by end-2029.
  • Against consensus: 6 percent tenancy growth plus 2 to 3 percent escalators less about 1 point of currency is about 7 to 9 percent revenue growth, matching consensus for FY2028 (7.0 percent) and above it for FY2029 (4.9 percent, two brokers); FY2027 consensus (10.4 percent) is above the informal run-rate because of the 2026 carry-over.
C-73

What else is material or uncommon about the company's guidance or investor relations?

C4510th of 30

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: several features of the guidance and investor relations are uncommon and matter for the model: the cash measure is before growth capex, the volume measure includes amendments, raises come paired with capex raises, and nothing below EBITDA is guided.

  • Recurring free cash flow is measured before discretionary capex, so it overstates cash available to equity in a high-capex year; in FY2026 it is about 220 to 235 USDm against discretionary capex of 215 to 245 USDm.
  • Tenancy additions are "points of service" and include lease amendments, which earn less than full colocations; per-tenancy revenue is therefore lower at the margin than the average.
  • Adjusted EBITDA is before lease costs; brokers value on EBITDAaL (Morgan Stanley's 7.4x 2028 EBITDAaL); the Consumer should keep the lease line separate.
  • Disclosure was reduced once (adjusted gross margin) and restated once (segment EBITDA) in a year when margin was debated.
  • Coverage is thin: three primary houses (Jefferies, Morgan Stanley, Deutsche Bank) plus Peel Hunt and New Street in FactSet; Morgan Stanley's estimates are not visible in FactSet's broker detail.
  • Investor relations is proactive: a technical white paper and adviser-led presentation on satellites; quarterly trading updates; a strategic finance and investor relations team formed for IMPACT 2030.

Judgement: operating estimates have not been cut but raised, and guidance has been raised, not rebased; EPS estimates have been cut deeply and repeatedly, but by the below-EBITDA lines, not by a kitchen-sink reset; the EPS bar is therefore lower than it was, but still rests on a tax normalisation that has not happened.

  • Raised: FY2027 EBITDA and revenue consensus +6 percent over six months; targets +24 percent over six months; every named broker raised EBITDA after the July results.
  • Cut: FY2026 EPS mean -19.2 percent over twelve months, -7.0 percent over six and -3.4 percent over three (from a peak of 0.238 to 0.089 USD); FY2028 -12.7 percent over twelve months; FY2025 fell from 0.207 to 0.033 USD before the result. FY2027 EPS is flat over one to six months (+1 percent over three and six months on the revision file), suggesting the cuts are slowing.
  • Breadth: four brokers span 0.054 (New Street) to 0.100 USD (Jefferies) for FY2026, so the EPS line is loosely modelled rather than uniformly cut.

Group judgement

Risks

C5411th of 30

Layer 3 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The financial forecasts for the next 18 months are highly predictable at every line the company reports, guides and is valued on by the market (revenue, Adjusted EBITDA, recurring free cash flow, leverage, distributions), with a cone of about ±4 percent on FY2027 EBITDA and an upward lean from a management that under-guides; they are poorly predictable at the line the model values (EPS), with a cone of about ±30 percent, a downward skew from one-sided currency and tax items, and a near-certain miss of consensus at the FY2026 print in March 2027. The earlier sets' work is congruent on the operating spine and jars only below operating profit, where the second set's finance and lease inputs were too light against the H1 2026 accounts; corrected, FY2026 net income is about 72 USDm (consensus 99.5) and FY2027 about 142 USDm (consensus 144.5). The change expected to FY2027 rests on demonstrated strengths; beyond it, two untested conditions (a post-surge normalisation and the first end-of-term renewals, now located in the DRC in 2028 to 2029) and three uncontrolled financial lines carry the EPS case. The drivers are concentrated in one operating mechanism (lease-up on a fixed base, driven by three operators' capex) and diffuse across four financial lines. Disclosure lets the operating case be tracked quarterly and the EPS case only at the two annual prints. The share price moves on company and frontier news, not with the sector, so delivery will be rewarded or punished on its own terms. For the memo: present the next 18 months as an operating certainty and an earnings lottery, value on the former, and date the risk to the latter (5 November 2026; March 2027).

D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

B+802nd of 30

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the expected change through FY2027 (revenue growth of about 10 to 11 percent, EBITDA growth of about 12 to 13 percent, a margin above 57 percent, recurring free cash flow of about 265 USDm, the completion of the 175 USDm buyback and a dividend growing over 10 percent a year) rests almost entirely on strengths the business has demonstrated for a decade; the change expected after FY2027 (EBITDA compounding at 7 to 9 percent through a demand normalisation and the first end-of-term renewals, and EPS tripling by FY2030) relies on two conditions the company has never faced and on three financial lines it does not control, so plausibility is high for the next 18 months and moderate beyond.

The expected change, line by line, against the demonstrated strength that carries it.

Expected change (model, FY2026 to FY2027) Demonstrated strength Evidence (dated) Strength the business would need to acquire
Tenancy additions about 4,100 then 3,000; ratio 2.26x to 2.44x Lease-up on an existing grid at the densest positions Ratio 2.09x (Q1 2025) to 2.26x (H1 2026); 2,511 additions in H1 2026 against 2,538 for all of FY2025; FY2023 additions 2,433 against 1,600 to 2,100 guided; about 38 percent of towers still single-tenant (Deutsche Bank, April 2026) A year of additions falling 25 to 30 percent after a surge: never experienced since the 2019 IPO

What the precedents say about the two untested conditions. On post-surge normalisation: the United States tower industry's leasing slowed to about 1 percent organic growth for American Tower after the 2018 to 2023 mid-band 5G build while EBITDA kept growing; India's operator capex fell from 927 INRbn (FY2024) to 596 INRbn (FY2026) yet Indus Towers added 7,900 colocations in one quarter of 2026 on Vodafone Idea's catch-up; American Tower's Africa and Asia-Pacific organic billings grew 8.2 percent in Q3 2026 against 1.1 percent consolidated. The pattern is that additions fall but do not collapse, and the installed base keeps earning; Helios's contract structure (non-cancellable, indexed) means revenue cannot fall, only grow more slowly (with zero additions, escalators alone give about 2 to 3 percent revenue growth). On renewals: every emerging-market precedent ended in a trade for term (American Tower and Airtel Africa, 12 years, cash-neutral; IHS and MTN Nigeria through 2032 with a 20 percent cap on naira CPI and diesel indexation; Sarana Menara, an 8 percent cut for 12 years), with one switching precedent (MTN moving about 2,500 Nigerian tenancies from IHS to American Tower from 2025, where an alternative grid existed). Morgan Stanley's October 2026 survey that 66 percent of tower operators report increased carrier pushback on lease terms (from 45 percent) is a United States reading but points the same way: the renewal will cost something, and the something is modest where density is dominant.

What the thesis does not need. It does not need pricing power (indexation only), new markets, acquisitions, cost cuts beyond the existing programme, or a lower cost of capital. It does not need the IMPACT 2030 targets to be raised to be met: over 42,000 tenancies is reached in H1 2029 on the normalisation path, the 2.5x ratio in 2028, cumulative recurring free cash flow of over 1.3 USDbn is exceeded by about 0.2 USDbn. The one target the surge makes harder to beat is the EBITDA CAGR of over 9 percent if it is measured from the inflated FY2026 base (about 8.8 percent on the model's path), which matters for the fourth set.

What the thesis does need that is not demonstrated. Three things, all below EBITDA: a tax charge that grows more slowly than pre-tax profit (plausible mechanically, undemonstrated in any year so far), a currency line that stays small (partly mitigated by the 2025 net-investment designation, not controlled), and a finance charge that holds near 180 USDm while 180 USDm of 2.875 percent paper is replaced at about 8 percent (plausible, demonstrated in part by the 2026 refinancings). The model's EPS path from 0.070 to 0.272 USD needs all three; the EBITDA and cash paths need none.

D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

D4514th of 30

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: over the next 18 months the cone is narrow and slightly upward-skewed for revenue, EBITDA and recurring free cash flow (about ±4 percent on FY2027 EBITDA), wide for free cash flow after growth capex (because capex and payables follow demand), and very wide and downward-skewed for reported earnings per share (about ±30 percent on FY2027, with the FY2026 print almost certain to miss consensus by a quarter or more); the case is therefore highly predictable in the form the company reports it and the sell-side values it (EBITDA, recurring cash, leverage) and poorly predictable in the form the model prices it (EPS at a P/E multiple), and the Consumer should carry both facts into the memo rather than average them.

The cone, line by line, to about April 2028 (FY2026 print, FY2027 in full, Q1 2027 to Q3 2027 updates and the opening of FY2028 guidance).

Why the operating cone is narrow. Three facts fix its width. The contracted base cannot fall: 5.9 USDbn of non-cancellable, indexed revenue with 6.5 years remaining and a 1 percent annual churn cap means the downside is to growth, not to the level (zero additions would still give about 2 to 3 percent revenue growth and 4 to 5 percent EBITDA growth from escalators and fixed costs). The 2026 additions are already signed: whatever H2 2026 and FY2027 bring, the roughly 2,500 tenancies added in H1 2026 earn a full year in 2027 on sites already costed. And the company guides conservatively and has beaten every operating line for three years; the probability that FY2027 EBITDA lands below 578 USDm requires either additions under 2,000 in FY2027 (a halving from the normalisation path, which Airtel's held 1.1 USDbn guide and Vodacom's 13.0 to 14.5 percent capex intensity do not suggest) or a devaluation of about 20 percent across the non-pegged markets (about 14 USDm per 10 percent).

Why the EPS cone is wide and skewed down. Four lines sit between EBITDA and EPS and none is guided. Finance: fixed-rate debt makes the underlying charge predictable, but the H1 2026 run-rate (about 101 USDm before currency) was 15 to 20 USDm a half above what the second set modelled, and the currency element within finance costs has ranged from +18.9 USDm (H1 2025) to -86.1 USDm (FY2023) in the last four years. Tax: the interim rate of 45.6 percent, the FY2025 rate of 71 percent and a disclosed 64.0 USDm deferred-asset unwinding show that a single year's charge can move by 30 to 60 USDm on items unconnected to operations; management's cash-tax guide (5 to 6 percent of revenue) steadies the cash but not the charge. Currency: the 2025 net-investment designation reduces but does not remove the exposure; the direction of frontier currencies is asymmetric. Share count: predictable for 2027, then a policy choice. Each of these can take 10 to 30 percent off a year's reported EPS, and the ones that are one-sided (currency, audits) are the ones most likely to surprise; hence a cone of roughly ±30 percent with more mass below the centre than above.

The dated events that will narrow the cone, in order. 5 November 2026 (Q3 additions above about 800 and guidance tightened to the upper end would move FY2026 to the top of the operating cone; additions below about 600 would widen the FY2027 cone downward); Airtel Africa's third-quarter results (early 2027: a quarter at about 220 USDm of capex confirms normalisation at the pace assumed, a quarter near 330 USDm raises the FY2027 volume case); the FY2026 results in March 2027 (the first full-year EPS print, the FY2026 tax charge, FY2027 EBITDA guidance against 585 to 600, the convertible's cash redemption); the DRC's cash rule on 9 April 2027 (a test of collections and supplier payments, probably passed); the H1 2027 results in July 2027 (the growth peak, about 11 percent revenue growth, and the first half with a full-year carry of 2026); the Q3 2027 update (the first reported deceleration); the FY2027 results in March 2028 (FY2028 guidance and the state of DRC renewal talks).

So how predictable is the case? For the lines the company reports and guides, more predictable than almost any other business in the sector: a tower company with indexed ten-year contracts, a 1 percent churn cap and a management that under-guides. For the line the model values, less predictable than the sector average, because frontier taxation and currency add noise that European towers do not carry. The practical implication for the memo is that the return of record (+127 percent to December 2027 at 26.28x two-year-forward EPS) inherits the wide cone, whereas a return struck on EV/EBITDA or recurring free cash flow would inherit the narrow one; the memo Analyst should say which cone the return rests on.

D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

B+603rd of 30

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: disclosure is unusually helpful for monitoring the operating drivers over the next six months (quarterly tenancies, sites and ratios by market, building-block guidance, rules of thumb, recurring free cash flow and leverage at each half) and unhelpful for monitoring the lines that will decide the next EPS print (no profit, tax, finance, currency or cash-flow disclosure at the quarterly updates, no guidance below EBITDA, one measure withdrawn and one restated in 2026); customers' disclosure fills part of the gap on demand but nothing fills it below EBITDA until March 2027.

What the next six months' prints will show, and when.

  • 5 November 2026, Q3 trading update: tenancy additions by market, sites, tenancy ratios, revenue and Adjusted EBITDA for the quarter (consensus 134.7 USDm), any change to FY2026 guidance (last year management tightened the range to the upper end at Q3), capex guidance, and commentary on the 2027 pipeline. No profit, tax, finance cost, cash flow, working capital, capital work in progress or net debt figure is expected at a trading update.
  • Airtel Africa, early 2027 (quarter to December 2026): quarterly capex against about 220 USDm implied by its held full-year guide; sites added; its 4G and 5G site counts (99.2 percent of sites 4G-enabled, 4,094 5G sites at September 2026). This is the best external reading of H1 2027 demand.
  • Vodacom, November 2026 (interim to September 2026): capex intensity against 13.0 to 14.5 percent; Tanzania and DRC subscriber growth (16 and 11 percent in the prior year); any statement on Esengo Towers' build.
  • Orange, February 2027: Middle East and Africa eCAPEX against about 1.6 EURbn; Senegal's 5G obligations (500 sites by 2026).
  • March 2027, FY2026 results: the full income statement, the tax charge and reconciliation, the finance cost breakdown, cash flow, capex payables and capital work in progress, net debt, the contracted-revenue schedule, FY2027 guidance, the final dividend, and the convertible's settlement. This is the first point at which the EPS bridge can be checked.

The helpful parts, and why they matter. The company publishes the chain that drives EBITDA at a granularity few peers match: tenancies and sites per country each quarter; the ratio per country; hard-currency share by market (69 percent of revenue, 71 percent of EBITDA); the split of capex into discretionary and non-discretionary with a stated return gate (about 35 percent incremental ROIC); rules of thumb that convert volume into profit (about 5 USDm of in-year operating profit per 500 tenancies; 80 percent flow-through; over 10 USDm of annualised EBITDA from the 500 tenancies added to guidance in July 2026); escalator mechanics and timing (December to February); and a three-item guidance set (EBITDA, additions, recurring free cash flow) with a fourth (discretionary capex). With these, a quarterly tenancy number can be turned into a FY2027 EBITDA estimate within about 2 percent, which is why the brokers' EBITDA forecasts cluster within 3 percent of each other (581 to 596 USDm for FY2027).

The unhelpful parts, and why they matter more now. (1) Nothing below EBITDA is guided or explained in detail: no tax-rate guide (only the capital-markets-day cash-tax range of 5 to 6 percent of revenue), no finance-cost guide, no breakdown of the 125.8 USDm H1 2026 finance costs into interest, lease interest, accretion, fees and currency, no EPS guide. (2) Adjusted gross margin was withdrawn as a disclosed measure in FY2025, exactly when power pass-through began diluting the margin percentage; segment EBITDA was restated for corporate recharges in Q1 2026. (3) Quarterly updates carry no cash flow, so the unwinding of the capex payables (creditor days 52 at June 2026 against 32 at December 2025; trade and other payables 467 USDm against 384) and the conversion of capital work in progress (163 USDm at end-2025, not disclosed in the interim) cannot be tracked until March 2027. (4) Renewal progress is not disclosed; Jefferies has asked management directly for the share of agreements due and the state of pricing talks and has received no public answer; the contracted-revenue schedule is the only window. (5) The brokers' EPS basis is itself unclear (FactSet records the FY2025 actual at 0.033 USD, the diluted reported figure; four houses span 0.054 to 0.100 USD for FY2026), so even a clean print may be reported as a miss or a beat depending on the basis chosen. (6) The company does not disclose intercompany loan balances by subsidiary or the share designated as net investment, so the currency exposure cannot be sized from outside.

Implication for monitoring. The Consumer can monitor the operating thesis quarterly with high confidence and should set the 5 November thresholds accordingly (additions, guidance range, pipeline language). The EPS thesis cannot be monitored until March 2027, and the first thing to look for then is the finance-cost note and the tax reconciliation, not the EBITDA. The memo should warn Richard that the March 2027 print carries the largest information release in the 18 months and that its EPS line will read badly against consensus even if the operating lines beat.

D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

C+558th of 30

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the company's stated targets trace cleanly onto operational drivers and onto revenue, EBITDA and recurring free cash flow (tenancies, ratio, flow-through, capex gate, distributions), and the external transmission from operators' capex to those lines is explicit and dated; the trace breaks at operating profit, because the company says nothing about finance, tax, currency or share count and the brokers and FactSet disagree about the EPS basis, so the route from value-chain dynamics to EPS is clear for two thirds of its length and opaque for the last third, which is the third the model values.

The internal trace, from what the company says it will achieve to the lines.

A worked bridge, for the Consumer's own use. 1,000 additional tenancies in a year add about 10 USDm of revenue in-year and about 28 USDm annualised; at an 80 percent flow-through that is about 8 USDm of in-year EBITDA and about 20 USDm annualised; depreciation is unaffected for colocations (about 10k USD of capex each) and about 2 to 3 USDm a year for the build share; the finance charge is unaffected; tax at an operating-company rate of about 30 percent takes about 6 USDm; so annualised net income rises about 12 to 14 USDm, which on about 1,000m shares is about 0.013 USD of EPS, about 9 percent of FY2027 EPS. The same 1,000 tenancies are about 3.4 percent of FY2027 EBITDA. The gearing of EPS to volume is therefore about 2.7 times, which is the operating leverage and financial leverage compounding; it works identically in reverse.

The external transmission. From value chain to tenancies it is explicit, measurable and dated: operators' capex (Airtel Africa quarterly; Vodacom semi-annually with a stated intensity target; Orange annually with a stated Middle East and Africa envelope; Omantel's 5G and 3G retirement) drives 4G densification and coverage builds; Helios reports the result per market each quarter; the time lag is one to two quarters. From tenancies to EBITDA it is mechanical. From EBITDA to EPS it runs through the balance sheet and the tax system of nine countries and a Mauritian holding structure, none of which the value chain touches: a devaluation in Malawi (3 percent of revenue) can cost more reported profit than 500 tenancies add. That is why the sell-side's revisions to EBITDA (+6 percent for FY2027 over six months) and to EPS (-8 percent over twelve months) have moved in opposite directions on the same operating news.

Two further breaks the memo should name. First, the four EPS bases (reported basic, FactSet adjusted diluted, the Viewforth definition, the brokers' adjusted) make the last link ambiguous even in hindsight: the company's FY2025 basic EPS was 0.037 USD, its diluted 0.033, FactSet's "brokers' basis" actual 0.033, FactSet's adjusted diluted 0.038. Second, management does not present EPS at all; its headline per-share measure is "diluted Adjusted EBITDA per share" (39.9 cents in 2025) and recurring free cash flow per share (19.7 cents), which is the clearest possible signal that the company intends to be valued on cash and EBITDA, not on earnings.

  • Supply: Newlight's remaining 4.9 percent and Quantum's stake.
  • Read-across from customers: a sequential fall in Airtel's capex (implied by its own guidance) could be read as a cut.
  • Re-integration narrative: MTN's completion of the IHS deal.

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

A841st of 30

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: the company and its chief executive upgrade guidance habitually, incrementally and credibly: conservative opening ranges, raised after delivery, with volume raised a lot and EBITDA a little, and capex raised alongside; it is a "sandbagging precision" pattern, not a promotional one.

  • Guidance midpoints raised in each of FY2023, FY2024 and FY2025; twice in FY2026 (May and July); FY2023 tenancy additions 31.5 percent above the top of the opening range; the 2.2x tenancy ratio target reached a year early.
  • The shape of the 2026 raises: tenancy additions +67 percent on the opening midpoint, EBITDA +2 percent, recurring free cash flow +5 USDm, discretionary capex +35 USDm at the midpoint. The EBITDA effect of the volume arrives in FY2027.
  • The chief executive's stated posture is conservative initial guidance; the leadership research and sell-side both describe "under-promise and deliver".
  • Caution: upgrades are small in dollars on EBITDA and cash; the market did not reward the July raise (the shares fell over the following three months).
C-69

What is the company's record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over the last one, three and five years, including in comparable environmental conditions?

C-5213th of 30

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: over one, three and five years the company has met or beaten its guidance and consensus on every operating line, by small margins on EBITDA and large ones on volumes, including in hostile conditions; on the equity line (EPS) consensus has been missed repeatedly and by large margins.

  • One year: FY2025 Adjusted EBITDA 471.1 USDm against 470 USDm guidance and 465 USDm prior consensus (+1.3 percent; revenue 854.1 USDm against 848 USDm, +0.7 percent); Q1 2026 revenue +3.8 percent and EBITDA +1.9 percent against company-compiled consensus, tenancy additions +150 percent; Q2 2026 revenue +0.2 percent, EBITDA +0.8 to 0.9 percent, tenancy additions +127 percent. FY2025 EPS consensus fell 51.7 percent over the twelve months before the result.
  • Three years: FY2023 EBITDA 370 USDm against 350 to 365 USDm guidance; tenancy additions 2,433 against 1,600 to 2,100; FY2024 EBITDA about 1.5 percent above consensus; fourteen consecutive quarterly EBITDA beats by mid-2025; guidance midpoints raised every year.
  • Five years and comparable conditions: ten consecutive years of EBITDA growth through Ghana's default, the 2022 to 2023 rate shock and frontier devaluations; but in 2022 to 2023 the company also missed on the equity line (losses, leverage peaking at 5.1x, delays closing Oman) and the shares fell 45 percent.
  • Implication for set three: operating forecasts deserve high weight and an upward lean; EPS forecasts deserve low weight and a downward lean on their level.
P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

A622nd of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the track record justifies high weight on the operating forecasts (revenue, EBITDA, tenancies, recurring free cash flow) with a modest upward lean over the next 18 months, medium weight on capex and free cash flow after growth capex with a lean toward higher capex, and low weight on EPS with a downward lean in FY2026 only; beyond 18 months the record is silent on the one condition that matters most, a post-surge normalisation followed by renewals, so the FY2028 to FY2030 forecasts deserve less weight and no upward lean.

Why the EPS lean flips between FY2026 and FY2027. The record of EPS misses is a record of the below-EBITDA lines surprising. In FY2026 three of them are already locked against consensus: H1 profit was only 21.7 USDm (19.7 USDm attributable) after a 21.8 USDm currency loss on intercompany loans, which (on my reading of FactSet's actuals, see the reading notes) counts against consensus; the interim tax charge is struck at the forecast full-year rate, which was high; and H2 needs about 80 USDm of net income for consensus 99.5 USDm, against about 64 USDm on my numbers. In FY2027 the levers move the other way: no currency loss is forecast by anyone, the convertible's maturity removes about 61.5m potential shares that brokers still count, and the buyback continues. The lean is therefore not "EPS always disappoints"; it is "the EPS level is a residual, so weight the components, not the history of the total".

What the record does not cover. Since the 2019 IPO Helios has never faced (1) a year after a demand surge in which additions fall by a quarter or more, or (2) a renewal of a major cohort at the end of its initial term. Its 2023 to 2025 beats came in a steady 2,000 to 2,500 additions regime with a weaker input force. Both untested conditions arrive in FY2027 to FY2028. The record therefore supports high weight on the level of EBITDA (contracted, indexed) but not on the FY2028 growth rate, which is where I place no upward lean and a small renewal concession.

Peers. American Tower's African and Asia-Pacific organic billings (8 to 11 percent) and IHS's tenancy ratio (1.47 to 1.51x) show that Helios out-executes; the peer record of renewals (IHS/MTN, Indus, Sarana Menara, Cellnex/MasOrange) shows concessions traded for term, which is the basis of my modal renewal outcome.

Group judgement

Financial forecasts

A701st of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The operating and financial model is unusually well anchored at the EBITDA and recurring-cash level and unusually loosely anchored at the EPS level, and its shape over four years is set by one fact: growth now lands on a fixed cost and capital base, so a halving of revenue growth from about 11 percent (H1 2027) to about 5.5 percent (FY2029) still delivers rising margins, a free cash flow swing of about 270 USDm from FY2026 to FY2030 and basic EPS that more than triples (0.080 to 0.280 USD). For the Consumer, the forecast's predictability splits cleanly: revenue, EBITDA, recurring free cash flow and the FY2027 to FY2028 tenancy path are contracted, indexed and guided by a management that beats, and their 18-month cone is about ±4 percent; free cash flow after growth capex depends on a capex line that follows demand and lags it by a year; EPS depends on four below-EBIT inputs, of which two (the share count and the finance charge) favour consensus being beaten in FY2027 to FY2029, and two (tax and currency on intercompany loans) make FY2026 a probable miss and leave every year exposed to a negatively skewed, unforecastable currency line. The main thing the market has not yet absorbed is the timing: reported growth peaks in H1 2027, the Q3 2026 update will show additions falling from Q2's level, and the FY2026 EPS print will likely disappoint, even as FY2027 EBITDA guidance is likely to be set at or above consensus and the cash and buyback story strengthens; the main thing the forecasts cannot yet know is the outcome of the 2027 to 2028 renewals, which I have modelled as a modest give and which is the largest fundamental downside beyond 18 months.

P-08

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

B646th of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: below operating profit the next four years look very different from the last six: the finance charge stops growing, the tax rate falls mechanically from about 46 percent (FY2026) to about 30 percent (FY2030) as operating profit outgrows the tax-friction base, currency losses on intercompany loans become the main source of EPS surprise, and buybacks shrink the share count by about 16 percent; net income rises from 39 USDm (FY2025) to about 250 USDm (FY2030) and free cash flow from about nil to about 240 USDm, but FY2026 is a weak transition year for both.

Tax: I partly disagree with the first set. The first set called consensus's falling tax rate "the least credible line". I judge the decline from FY2027 mechanically plausible, because the tax charge is driven by operating-company profits and withholding taxes (it scales roughly with EBITDA), while pre-tax profit grows much faster because interest and depreciation are fixed. Cash tax was 7.9 percent of EBITDA in FY2024 and 9.7 percent in FY2025; I let it rise to about 13 percent by FY2030 (more operating companies exhausting losses, DRC minimum taxes, upstreaming) plus about 12 USDm a year of deferred tax (tax depreciation running ahead of 30-year book lives). Even so the rate on pre-tax profit falls from about 46 percent to about 30 percent. The structural frictions are real (holding-company losses in Mauritius and the UK cannot be group-relieved, DRC minimum tax applies in loss years, withholding taxes on upstreaming; Airtel Africa's rate is 40.2 percent against a 32 percent statutory average for the same reasons), but they are a roughly fixed amount, not a rate. The tax risk is concentrated in FY2026 (an interim charge struck at a high forecast full-year rate, and the possibility of a further DRC deferred-tax unwind like FY2025's) and in one-off swings, not in the trend. A rate 5 points above mine in FY2028 costs about 14 USDm of net income (about 8 percent).

Currency on intercompany loans: the main surprise line. Subsidiaries carry dollar loans from the parent; when the Tanzanian shilling, Malawi kwacha, Ghanaian cedi or Malagasy ariary weaken, the subsidiary books a non-cash loss. It cost 86 USDm in FY2023 and 21.8 USDm in H1 2026, and it is not forecast by anyone. The skew is negative: frontier currencies depreciate more often than they appreciate (the cedi fell 12.1 percent in the first nine months of 2026), so the expected value is a loss of perhaps 10 to 20 USDm a year, with a tail of 50 to 90 USDm in a devaluation year. I assume zero to stay comparable with consensus and flag it as the largest single downside to every year's EPS.

One-offs. Unprovided tax assessments total about 104 to 106 USDm (DRC environmental tax 39.9, DRC 26.9 to 27.2, Congo Brazzaville 22.3 plus 6.5 to 7.9, Tanzania 8.8 to 9.4 USDm); Orange RDC paid 25 EURm to close a DRC audit, which argues that a part will be paid. I expect a settlement of perhaps 10 to 30 USDm of cash over four years, probably booked as an exceptional item (brokers would then exclude it, but FactSet's reported basis would not). Unrecognised contract revenue (46.8 USDm, up 149 percent, a key audit matter) is not in the P&L, so it is upside if collected and a working-capital and credit warning if it keeps rising (tripwire 55 USDm).

Buybacks and issuance. The 175 USDm programme had about 115 USDm left in July 2026; I assume about 52 USDm of purchases in FY2026 and 99 USDm in FY2027 to finish it, then new programmes of 110, 150 and 180 USDm (FY2028 to FY2030) as leverage falls toward the bottom of the 2.5x to 3.5x corridor; these are my assumption, consistent with IMPACT 2030's "at least 250 USDm of buybacks and 150 USDm of dividends" and with Deutsche Bank's view that over 800 USDm is returnable. Share plans add about 2m shares a year (FactSet's share count was flat at about 1,050m from FY2022 to FY2024, so plans have been met largely without new issuance). The proposal to remove the 5 percent discretionary share-plan cap leaves the 10 percent limit and is a permission, not a forecast. The convertible is redeemed in cash in my base case (the share price is below the about 2.93 USD conversion price).

Free cash flow, against the past. FactSet's free cash flow (before leases and intangibles) went from -192 USDm (FY2022) to +90.6 USDm (FY2025). On my measure (after leases and all capex, before distributions and acquisitions) FY2025 was about +30 USDm; FY2026 falls to about -30 USDm because capex rises about 100 USDm and working capital absorbs about 27 USDm, then FY2027 to FY2030 rise to about 100 to 240 USDm as capex normalises. The company's recurring free cash flow (before discretionary capex and working capital) runs about 227, 265, 300, 334 and 372 USDm, about 1.5 USDbn in total for FY2026 to FY2030, in line with Jefferies and above the company's over 1.3 USDbn target.

For set three. Net income and EPS rest on four below-EBIT inputs with very different predictability: finance (high: fixed-rate debt to 2029 and 2031), share count (high for 2027, a policy choice after), tax (medium: trend predictable, level noisy), currency on intercompany loans (low: unforecastable, negatively skewed). The last two are where FY2026 and FY2027 reported EPS can miss.

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: beyond cost cuts there is substantial, plausible and strategy-congruent scope to improve the financial outputs over the mid-term, mostly from a few big areas (lease-up of the existing grid, especially newer markets) supported by many small ones; peer precedents show it is achievable, and the one area that is not in the company's control is the EBITDA-to-EPS drop-through.

C-80

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

D5516th of 30

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: the core growth engine has not decelerated over the last twelve months; it accelerated, with revenue growth above the company's normal rate and tenancy additions at a record; but the second derivative has turned (Q2 below Q1, guidance implying a halving in H2), and the input force itself, customers' capex, is set to slow sequentially, so a visible deceleration in additions is probable within six months even as reported revenue growth holds.

  • Revenue growth: 7.3 percent (H1 2025), 8.4 percent (H2 2025), 11.5 percent (H1 2026), against the company's FY2024 to FY2025 normal of 8 to 10 percent.

  • Implication for later sets: the market may read normalisation as a slowdown; the Q3 trading update (5 November 2026) and Airtel's February 2027 results are the tests.
C-67

How do the sell-side's forecasts for the next one to three years compare with the last one to five years, for the main financial statement line items in particular and the granular working up to them, and how credible is the gap given the change forces and foundations found above?

C+509th of 30

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: the sell-side's forecasts extend the recent operating trend credibly (revenue about 10 percent for two years then 7 percent, EBITDA 12 percent then 9 percent, margin up about a point a year), with modest upside on volumes and FY2028 revenue; the gap that is not credible as it stands is below EBIT, where consensus assumes a sharp fall in the tax rate and a flat finance charge, and in the capex line, which assumes demand normalises quickly.

Line by line, last three years against the next three (consensus medians, four brokers):

  • Revenue growth: 28.6 percent (FY2023, Oman), 9.8 percent, 7.8 percent; then 10.9, 10.4, 7.0 percent (4.9 percent in FY2029 on two brokers). Credible; FY2028 to FY2029 conservative if management's 6 percent tenancy growth holds.
  • EBITDA (brokers' basis): 370, 421, 471 USDm (growth 13.8 and 11.9 percent); then 528, 594, 647, 699 USDm. Margin 55.2 percent to 55.7, 56.8, 57.8 and 59.6 percent; incremental margins 67 to 73 percent. Credible, slight upside in FY2026.
  • Depreciation and EBIT: depreciation about flat (168, 180, 184 USDm) after the 2024 life extension; EBIT margin 34.2 percent to 35.6, 38.4 and 39.9 percent. Depreciation rises faster if capex stays high.
  • Finance charge: about 173, 184 and 182 USDm implied (EBIT less pre-tax profit), roughly flat against 182 USDm net interest in FY2025 (FactSet). Risks upward: refinancing the low-coupon 2027 convertible; higher net debt if capex stays elevated; currency losses on intercompany loans, which brokers exclude but which hit reported profit.
  • Tax rate (with minorities): about 39, 34 and 31 percent implied, against 38.9 percent (FY2024) and 71 percent (FY2025) reported. The least credible line; a 40 percent rate in FY2028 takes about 13 percent off consensus net income.
  • Net income and EPS: 99.5, 144, 183 USDm; 0.090, 0.130, 0.170 USD. Consensus appears to divide by about 1.10bn shares (diluted for the convertible) against about 1.04bn in issue with a buyback running: modest upside from the share count.
  • Capex: 144 (FY2024) and 180 USDm (FY2025); then 278, 168, 160 USDm. The FY2027 fall assumes discretionary capex roughly halves; if additions stay above 3,000 it will not, and free cash flow and deleveraging will be slower while EBITDA growth is faster.
  • Dividend: 0.020, 0.027, 0.027 USD a share, consistent with 25 USDm growing over 10 percent; the flat FY2028 figure looks conservative.
  • Leverage (my arithmetic, not consensus): about 3.3x to 3.4x on the company's basis at end-FY2026, about 3.0x at end-FY2027 and about 2.6x at end-FY2028 if capex normalises as consensus assumes.

P-09

After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

A751st of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the net effect is a business whose top line grows at about half its last-six-year rate but whose margins, operating profit, net income, free cash flow and per-share figures improve far faster than in the past, because growth now lands on a fixed cost and capital base instead of being bought with acquisitions and debt; the inflection is FY2027, and the main lines' growth rates all decelerate from FY2028.

The comparison, main lines (history from FactSet and the company; forecasts my base case).
