The joined-up reading.

  • Growth halves, quality rises. FY2019 to FY2025 growth was 14 percent a year with about half from acquisitions that earned about 6 percent ROIC at entry (8.5 percent by mid-2025) against an 11 percent WACC, and that drove leverage to 5.1x. FY2025 to FY2030 growth is about 7.8 percent a year, all organic, at incremental returns of about 20 percent pre-tax on new builds and well over 100 percent on colocations.
  • Every line below revenue accelerates relative to revenue. The ratio of EBITDA growth to revenue growth is about 1.2 (9.5 percent against 7.8 percent); of operating profit growth about 1.6; of net income growth about 6. This is the double leverage of P-01 and P-02 working on a now-stable base.
  • The cash profile changes sign permanently. The company spent more than its operating cash flow every year from FY2019 to FY2023; from FY2027 it generates free cash flow of about 10 to 20 percent of revenue after all capex and leases, which is the precondition for the buybacks that cut the share count.
  • The deceleration is unavoidable and should be expected by the market. Revenue growth: 11.0, 10.0, 6.8, 5.5 and 5.6 percent; EBITDA growth 12.7, 12.6, 8.7, 6.8 and 7.2 percent; EPS growth (basic) about 117, 90, 25, 20 and 22 percent. From FY2028 Helios looks like a mid-single-digit revenue, high-single-digit EBITDA, high-teens EPS and FCF-per-share compounder, not the double-digit grower of 2026 and 2027.

Against consensus (the gaps the Consumer will test). Revenue and EBITDA within about 1 percent in every year to FY2028; FY2029 revenue and EBITDA about 0 to 1 percent from the two-broker figures; net income -17 percent (FY2026), +6 percent (FY2027), +1 percent (FY2028), -3 percent (FY2029); EPS on a like-for-like diluted basis -18 percent (FY2026: 0.073 against 0.090 USD), +12 percent (FY2027: 0.146 against 0.130), +8 percent (FY2028: 0.184 against 0.170), +11 percent (FY2029: 0.221 against 0.200, two brokers). The FY2026 gap is the currency loss, the high H1 tax charge and my lower H2 profit; the FY2027 to FY2029 gaps are mainly the share count (consensus keeps about 1.08bn to 1.11bn shares), plus a lower finance charge in FY2027. Capex: mine 285, 200 and 175 USDm against consensus 278, 168 and 160 USDm, so my free cash flow is lower than consensus in FY2027 and FY2028.

Forecasts and upgrades

Probably upgrades this financial year to

B+563rd of 30

Memo topic 7, Scope for upgrades over 6 to 12 months: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

B526th of 30

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Company guidance reflects less than we expect on every operating line and will be raised or tightened upward at most of the six events in the next 18 months; sell-side estimates reflect our operating view almost exactly and our earnings view poorly, so EBITDA consensus will drift up while FY2026 EPS consensus will be cut by a quarter or more at the March 2027 print and the later years will be held; and the narrative is a four-broker, all-Buy, operating-and-cash story that does not articulate the below-EBITDA drags, the DRC concentration of the coming renewals or the timing of the growth peak, with no investor voice outside it. The gap between our assessment and what the market already reflects is therefore favourable on the lines the company reports and the market values (guidance will be beaten, EBITDA estimates will rise, cash returns will scale, and the price at about 8x EBITDA and the 44th percentile of its own P/E history reflects a frontier discount rather than the operating certainty), unfavourable on the line the model prices and the sell-side nominally forecasts (EPS), and sequenced so that the unfavourable part arrives first. Two features make the gap less exploitable than it looks: the favourable pattern (under-promise and over-deliver) is already the consensus description of the company and did not move the price in July 2026, and the share trades on its own news and on frontier sentiment rather than with the sector, so the reward for delivery depends on an unmodelled Africa factor as much as on the prints. The memo should therefore state that the case is well understood by the few who cover it, not understood at all below the operating line, and unowned by the wider market; that the next two events test the market's tolerance for an earnings line that will disappoint while the operating line beats; and that the favourable expectation gap is in the cash and per-share figures of 2027 to 2029, not in the next six months.

I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

A801st of 30

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement: at every earnings event in the next 18 months the operating guidance is more likely to be raised or tightened upward than cut, by a wide margin (about 60 to 70 percent raised or tightened upward, 25 to 35 percent held, under 5 percent cut at each of the six events), because the company guides below its run-rate and 2026's additions are contracted into 2027; the one event with a real two-sided risk is the opening of FY2027 guidance in March 2027, which will probably be set at or just below consensus on EBITDA and well below the 2026 volume, and will read as a deceleration even though it is conservative; the mid-term IMPACT 2030 targets will be met early on tenancies, ratio, cash flow and distributions and are more likely to be raised than held within 18 months, with the EBITDA CAGR the one target that will be restated rather than raised.

The events, what is guided, and my probabilities.

Event (date) What is in play Raised or tightened upward Met or held Reduced What drives each outcome
Q3 2026 trading update (5 November 2026) FY2026: additions 3,500 to 4,000; EBITDA 520 to 535; recurring free cash flow 220 to 235; discretionary capex 215 to 245 60 percent (tightened to the upper end, as at Q3 2025, or additions raised to 3,800 to 4,200) 35 percent 5 percent Q3 additions: brokers expect +526 to +800; the CFO flagged about 750 new sites to complete in H2; the order pipeline "into FY2027" (July 2026). A raise needs Q3 additions above about 900; a hold is consistent with 700 to 900; a cut needs a currency or collection shock. Capex guidance is more likely raised than EBITDA (the volume costs cash before it earns)
FY2026 results and first FY2027 guidance (March 2027) FY2026 actual against guidance; FY2027 EBITDA, additions, recurring free cash flow, capex; dividend; convertible settlement FY2026 beaten at the top of each range: 75 percent. FY2027 EBITDA guidance opening above consensus 594: 25 percent FY2027 opening at 580 to 600 (at or just below consensus): 60 percent FY2027 opening below 580: 15 percent Management's pattern is an opening range it can beat; the carry of 2026 (about 25 to 30 USDm) makes 585 to 600 the natural floor. FY2027 additions guidance will probably be 2,000 to 2,500 or 2,500 to 3,000, below the 2026 run-rate and below my 3,000: an optical cut that is not a cut in earnings
Q1 2027 trading update (May 2027) FY2027 guidance 55 percent 40 percent 5 percent Q1 additions against a 500 to 700 implied run-rate; January escalators; the record of raising in May (2026)
H1 2027 results (July 2027) FY2027 guidance; interim dividend 65 percent 30 percent 5 percent The growth peak (about 11 percent revenue growth in H1 2027); raises in July in 2024, 2025 and 2026
Q3 2027 trading update (November 2027) FY2027 guidance 50 percent (tightened upward) 45 percent 5 percent The first quarter in which year-on-year additions fall sharply (against Q3 2026's level); a tightening is the likely form
FY2027 results and FY2028 guidance (March 2028) FY2027 actual; FY2028 guidance; DRC renewal status; a possible IMPACT 2030 refresh FY2027 beaten: 70 percent; FY2028 guidance at or above consensus (about 647): 50 percent 40 percent 10 percent FY2028 is the first year with no carry from a surge and the first with renewal exposure; management may open FY2028 EBITDA at 630 to 650 against consensus 647

Why "raised" is the modal path. Three structural reasons, all demonstrated. First, the guidance is built from a run-rate the company already has in hand: in July 2026 the FY2026 tenancy range implied 495 to 745 additions a quarter against 1,255 a quarter delivered in H1, and the EBITDA midpoint implied about 10 percent H2 growth against 14 percent delivered in H1. Second, the biggest driver of FY2027 EBITDA is contracted: about 2,500 tenancies added in H1 2026 earn a full year in 2027 whatever operators do next, which is worth about 25 to 30 USDm and takes FY2027 to about 580 USDm before any 2027 additions. Third, the company's own rules of thumb mean a volume raise mechanically lifts the next year's guidance (over 10 USDm of annualised EBITDA per 500 tenancies), so the raises of 2026 are the raises of 2027. The customers' guidance supports the floor: Airtel Africa held 1.1 USDbn of capex for its year to March 2027 and added about 4,200 sites in six months; Vodacom holds 13.0 to 14.5 percent intensity and prioritises Tanzania and the DRC; Orange's Middle East and Africa eCAPEX is at about 1.6 EURbn.

What would drive a cut, and how likely. A cut to EBITDA guidance has not happened since the IPO and would need one of: a currency collapse across the non-pegged markets (about 14 USDm per 10 percent; the cedi fell 12 percent in nine months of 2026 without a guidance change); a customer payment failure (unrecognised contract revenue of 46.8 USDm, tripwire 55 USDm; Axian the most exposed); a repatriation restriction in the DRC or Tanzania (the 9 April 2027 cash rule is the nearest event; collections are electronic); or a collapse in additions below 1,500 a year (Morgan Stanley's bear case). I put the joint probability of a cut at any event in 18 months at about 10 to 15 percent, concentrated in the second half of 2027 and in the recurring-free-cash-flow line (which carries cash tax and cash interest) rather than in EBITDA.

The mid-term targets (IMPACT 2030, November 2025), secondarily.

  • Over 42,000 tenancies by 2030: reached in H1 2029 on the normalisation path (36,000 at end-2026 plus 2,300 to 3,000 a year); even 1,500 a year reaches it by 2030. Will be raised, probably to about 45,000 to 48,000. Probability of a raise by March 2028: about 60 percent.
  • Ratio above 2.5x: reached during 2028. Raised with the tenancy target.
  • Organic EBITDA CAGR above 9 percent: 9.5 percent from the FY2025 base on the model's path, about 8.8 percent from the FY2026 base; the company will present it as met from 2025 and will not raise it, because the 2026 surge inflates the base. This is the target most likely to be "held", and the one where a cautious reader will see the deceleration.
  • Cumulative recurring free cash flow over 1.3 USDbn (2026 to 2030): about 1.5 USDbn on the second set's path; raised with the tenancy target, probably to over 1.5 USDbn.
  • Distributions of at least 400 USDm: already 175 USDm of buybacks to December 2027 plus dividends of about 25 to 28 USDm a year; a raise to 600 USDm or more as leverage nears 3.0x is likely within 18 months (Deutsche Bank models over 800 USDm returnable).
  • ROIC 15 to 20 percent by 2030: 14.4 percent at H1 2026; met early.
  • Leverage 2.5x to 3.5x: held; a lower corridor is possible once 3.0x is passed in 2027. Timing: the AlphaSense evidence (file 7) expects no formal refresh before late 2027 or 2028; the first set expected March 2027 or an investor day. My view: a partial raise (tenancies, cash, distributions) with the FY2026 results in March 2027 is about 35 percent likely, and a refresh by March 2028 about 60 percent; the refresh will not change the EBITDA CAGR.

How our assessment differs from what guidance reflects. Our FY2026 EBITDA (531) sits at the top of the range; our additions (4,100) above the range; our FY2027 EBITDA (598) above where FY2027 guidance will probably open; our tenancy path reaches the 2030 target a year or more early. Guidance therefore reflects less than we expect on every operating line, which is favourable for the case; the gap is well known to the sell-side (every house describes the under-promise pattern), so the favourable surprise is in the figures, not in the pattern.

Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: consensus revenue and EBITDA will be nudged up, not re-based, at each of the next few events (about 0.5 to 1.5 percent a time, cumulatively 2 to 4 percent for FY2027 by mid-2027), because the four brokers already model close to the company's run-rate and will follow the guidance raises; consensus FY2026 EPS will be cut by a quarter or more at or before the March 2027 print, because it rests on a finance charge and a tax rate the H1 2026 accounts have already contradicted; consensus FY2027 and FY2028 EPS will be roughly held, because the cut to the finance line is offset by the buyback the brokers have not yet modelled; and the net breadth of revisions will stay positive on EBITDA and mixed on EPS, with no room for rating upgrades (100 percent Buy) and a non-trivial chance of the first non-Buy rating in 18 months.

Where consensus sits now, and against what. FactSet medians (28 October 2026): revenue 947, 1,046, 1,119 and 1,174 USDm; EBITDA 528, 594, 647 and 699; net income 99.5, 144, 183 and 220; EPS 0.090, 0.130, 0.170 and 0.200 USD (FY2026 to FY2029; two brokers for FY2029). Twelve-month revisions for FY2027: EBITDA and sales +6 percent, EPS -8 percent, target price +24 percent over six months; the dashboard's revision-momentum score is 3 and the pinned sell-side earnings momentum C. The four houses: Jefferies (EPS 0.100, 0.150, 0.183; target 4.01 USD), Peel Hunt (0.091, 0.130, 0.171; 3.90), Deutsche Bank (0.078, 0.125, 0.167; 3.83), New Street (0.054, 0.098, 0.143; 3.18); mean target 3.84 USD, about 41 percent above the price. Jefferies values at an 11 percent WACC with a 300p base, 350p upside and 125p downside; Morgan Stanley (not in FactSet's detail) models 2,300 additions a year through 2030, margin to 57 percent, capex to 16 percent of sales, a bear of 110p at 6.0x EBITDA.

The net breadth of revisions. On EBITDA and revenue: positive and shallow (four houses within 3 percent of each other; every one raised after July 2026; the next raise follows the next guidance change). On EPS: negative for FY2026, neutral for FY2027 and FY2028 until the buyback is modelled, then mildly positive; the twelve-month EPS revision of -8 percent for FY2027 is slowing (+1 percent over six months), consistent with the cuts being largely done except for FY2026. On ratings: 100 percent Buy across four houses, so the breadth of ratings cannot improve; the three-month fall of 17 points in the buy share is a contributor dropping out, not a downgrade. The probability of a first Hold rating in 18 months I put at about 30 percent, most likely from New Street (the lowest target and EPS) after a FY2026 EPS miss or from a house applying the sector's bond-proxy view; it would be news for a thinly covered stock. The probability of a fifth or sixth house initiating is about the same, given the FTSE 250 membership, the buyback and the cash story.

Why the EPS line will diverge from the EBITDA line at the March 2027 print. The brokers' basis is reported diluted EPS (FactSet's FY2025 actual of 0.033 USD is net income 39.2 USDm over about 1,180m diluted shares), so the H1 2026 currency loss and any H2 loss count. Three of the four houses hold a finance charge or a tax rate the H1 accounts contradict: on the consensus pre-tax profit of 164 USDm and a 39 percent all-in rate, net income of 99.5 USDm requires an H2 pre-tax profit of about 124 USDm against about 60 USDm on the H1 run-rate of finance costs. The cut is therefore mechanical once the full-year accounts are published, and because it is mechanical the houses will present it as "below the line" and keep their Buy ratings and EBITDA forecasts; the price reaction will depend on whether the narrative has prepared investors for it, which the next question addresses.

18 months to three years out, secondarily. FY2029 consensus (two brokers) and the first FY2030 figures will be built in 2027 to 2028 from the IMPACT 2030 targets; the likely moves are up on revenue, EBITDA and distributions as the tenancy target is refreshed, held on margins (brokers already have 59.6 percent for FY2029), and up on EPS from the share count unless the DRC renewal or a devaluation intervenes. I expect FY2029 EBITDA consensus to settle near 690 to 700 USDm (mine 693) and FY2029 EPS near 0.21 to 0.22 USD (mine 0.211 diluted), so the outer-year gap between our assessment and consensus is small and slightly favourable on EPS, neutral on EBITDA.

How our assessment differs from what estimates reflect. On the operating lines, consensus reflects our view within 1 percent in every year to FY2028: no gap. On FY2026 EPS, consensus is about 40 percent above our figure: an unfavourable gap that will close with a cut. On FY2027 to FY2029 EPS, consensus is 2 to 6 percent below our figures: a small favourable gap from the share count. On free cash flow, consensus is above ours in FY2027 and FY2028 by 20 to 30 USDm: a small unfavourable gap. On distributions, consensus understates the dividend growth: small favourable. Net, the estimate picture is neutral to slightly unfavourable over the next six months and neutral to slightly favourable thereafter.

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

C559th of 30

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the share price moves almost entirely on its own: over the latest six months only 8 percent of its daily variance is explained by the European tower basket (correlation 0.27, beta 0.48), down from 20 to 27 percent in 2025, and it returned +3.9 percent while the basket fell 9.0 percent; the multiple is therefore company-driven and Africa-driven, not industry-driven, which is favourable for a case that rests on the company's own delivery and unfavourable only in that a European tower re-rating would not lift it; the practical corollary is that the multiple will answer to the company's prints and to frontier sentiment, so the fourth set's questions about guidance and estimates are the right questions.

The evidence.

  • Rolling six-month windows (file 19): correlation with the equal-weighted basket of Cellnex, Inwit and Rai Way 0.27 at 30 October 2026, beta 0.48, R-squared 8 percent, idiosyncratic share 92 percent. Against each peer: Cellnex 0.25, Inwit 0.22, Rai Way 0.16. Over 24 month-ends the R-squared ran 13 to 27 percent through September 2025, fell to 4 percent in October 2025 (the month before the capital markets day) and has stayed at 8 to 14 percent since. The decoupling is a year old and coincides with the company's shift to cash returns and with the European towers' own troubles (Inwit's anchor-tenant notices, Cellnex's leverage).
  • Returns: six months +3.9 percent against the basket's -9.0 percent; twelve months +36.1 percent against Cellnex -1.0, Inwit -14.9 and Rai Way -23.9 percent. In mid-October 2026 Helios fell 8.2 percent while Cellnex rose 6.9 percent.
  • The dashboard's technical reading is D (price 205p below the 50-day and 150-day averages near 208.6p and 208.8p, 1.4 percent above a 200-day average that has risen in all twelve months, 17 percent below the May 2026 high of 247.6p after the +21 percent Q1-update jump and the -23 percent summer give-back), relative strength at the 70th percentile of the universe. The peers are F, C and F.
  • The multiple: FactSet's 24-month-forward P/E 17.15x (23 October), at the 39th percentile of five years and the 44th of ten; EV/EBITDA about 8.5x FY2026 and 7.6x FY2027 consensus against Cellnex and Inwit on 14 to 16x historically and about 6x for IHS and Indus (and roughly the price MTN is paying for IHS). The peers' P/Es (Cellnex 181x, Inwit 19.2x, Rai Way 12.2x) are not comparable because of their different depreciation and leverage; the market's lens for Helios is EV/EBITDA and recurring free cash flow yield (Jefferies: about 9 percent for 2027).

What drives the price if not the sector. The first set measured a correlation of about 0.30 with Airtel Africa and documented the share trading with London-listed Africa names; Morgan Stanley (October 2026) wrote that Helios "trades in line with higher-risk African equities" and that "unless global emerging market sentiment shifts, this African market correlation will keep the valuation discount locked in place". The company's own events explain the large moves (+16 percent on the Q1 2026 update day; the fall after the July beat-and-raise was attributed by Deutsche Bank to pre-IPO holders' selling: Newlight Partners from 8.7 to 4.9 percent and then below 3 percent, Quantum below 10 percent). So the residual 92 percent is split between company news and a frontier-Africa factor that no file quantifies; the Consumer should ask for an Airtel Africa and MTN co-movement reading if the memo needs it.

Implications.

  • For the fair multiple: it cannot be inferred from European towers' history; the guideline's own ten-year percentiles of the stock's P/E (median 21.2x, 35th percentile 15.3x, today about 15.7x to 17.2x) are the relevant range, and the private-market reference for African towers (about 6x EBITDA) is the floor the first set used.
  • For the sector's Underweight tilt (the first set's C-07): it is less of a drag than it looked; a bond-proxy sell-off in Cellnex or Inwit has transmitted little in the last year, which also means the portfolio's sector discipline should not be read mechanically onto this holding.
  • For predictability: a company-driven multiple means the price will respond to the dated events in D-04 and to the fourth set's guidance and estimate dynamics, which makes the share price more predictable from the fundamentals than a sector-driven one would be; but the frontier factor (US 10-year near 5.1 percent, Ghana's reserves, Senegal's rating, the DRC's currency rule) is an unmodelled second driver with roughly the same weight as the company's own news.

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the eleven ratings page uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

A622nd of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the historical evidence is that tower companies outperform when three things coincide (rising tenancy ratios on a fixed cost base, falling leverage with buybacks, and a stable or falling cost of capital) and underperform on four triggers (rising rates, customer consolidation or churn, customer credit distress in emerging markets, and leveraged acquisitions at high prices); Helios currently fits the first two outperformance conditions strongly, fails the third, and carries the emerging-market credit and currency triggers at lower intensity than peers.

  • Outperformance precedents. The US towers' long run of outperformance in the 2000s and 2010s came from lease-up on a fixed cost base, falling cost of capital and steady buybacks (outside the files, common knowledge of the sector). Helios's own record: the shares returned about 83 percent in the twelve months to spring 2026 as leverage fell from 5.1x to 3.4x and free cash flow turned positive (FactSet free cash flow from -192 USDm in FY2022 to +90.6 USDm in FY2025), and they decoupled from every European peer.
  • Underperformance precedents.
  • Rates: tower operators have underperformed traditional telecoms by 80 percent over five years (Morgan Stanley, September 2026); Cellnex fell from about 60 EUR to about 30 EUR as rates rose and leverage peaked at 8.4x.
  • Customer consolidation and churn: American Tower's US organic growth fell to about 1 percent with DISH churn; Inwit's anchors served termination notices (2026); MasOrange's merger let it remove 1,500 Cellnex points of presence.
  • Emerging-market customer distress: outside the files, American Tower's Indian tenant Vodafone Idea fell behind on payments in 2022 to 2023, forcing large reserves and impairments; American Tower then sold India for about 2.5 USDbn (the sale is in the files). Helios's analogue is the 46.8 USDm of unrecognised contract revenue from customer disputes and liquidity issues.
  • Currency: IHS, with 62 percent of revenue in Nigerian naira, saw its equity collapse through the 2023 to 2024 devaluations (the collapse is outside the files; the naira exposure is in them). Helios has 71 percent of EBITDA in hard or pegged currency.
  • Leverage and acquisitions: Helios's own 45 percent fall in 2023 came with 5.1x leverage, Ghana's default and delays closing Oman.
  • Fit today. Lease-up: yes, best in sector (EBITDA growth 13 to 14 percent against about 5 percent for European towers). Deleveraging and buybacks: yes. Cost of capital: adverse (US 10-year near 5.1 percent, frontier risk premium, an 11 percent WACC in broker models). Customer distress: low but non-zero (receivables concentration, unrecognised revenue). Currency: contained. A rule of thumb the sector digest adds: "A stock every analyst rates Overweight has no upgrades left"; Helios is 100 percent Buy.
  • Implication for the model and for later sets: operational outperformance is probable; share-price outperformance needs either a lower discount rate or a visible step-up in distributions, because the earnings beat alone has stopped moving the shares (the shares fell after the July beat-and-raise).

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

D4516th of 30

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: the shares could fall about 15 to 30 percent to what I judge a fair trough multiple on forward earnings power, and about 45 percent in a full emerging-market stress; leverage roughly doubles the equity's sensitivity to the enterprise multiple, so the multiple, not the earnings, is the source of the downside.

All against 205.2p (about 2.75 USD), market value about 2,867 USDm, net debt plus minorities about 1,631 USDm on FactSet's enterprise-value basis.

  • 24-month-forward P/E: today 17.15x; the two-year median 14.34x and the five-year 25th percentile 13.48x give -16 to -21 percent; the five-year 10th percentile 9.82x gives about -43 percent. The longer histories are distorted by years of negative earnings.
  • EV/EBITDA (the measure the sell-side uses): today 8.5x FY2026 and 7.6x FY2027 consensus EBITDA. At 7.0x FY2027 (594 USDm): equity about 2,527 USDm, -12 percent. At 6.5x FY2027 (Morgan Stanley's bear multiple): about 2,230 USDm, -22 percent. At 6.0x FY2026 (IHS, Indus Towers, and roughly the multiple MTN is paying for IHS): about 1,537 USDm, -46 percent.
  • Broker bear cases: Morgan Stanley 110p (-46 percent); Jefferies 125p (-39 percent).
  • My fair trough: 6.5x to 7.0x forward EBITDA, between the private-market reference for African towers (about 6x) and today's 8.5x, giving about 145p to 180p, -12 to -29 percent; the 6x stress is the tail. Each year of 10 percent EBITDA growth lifts the trough value by about 15 to 20 percent because of the leverage.

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

C+548th of 30

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The narrative ahead is a steady, credible staircase of a compounder harvesting its footprint: beats on volumes, raised guidance, rising distributions, falling leverage and, within eighteen months, refreshed long-term targets. Its weakness is novelty: the market already knows the story (100 percent Buy, targets about 40 percent above the price) and did not reward the last beat; the steps that could move the multiple are the distribution step-up and the target refresh, while the nearest dated events (a sequential fall in additions, customers' capex normalising, the DRC currency rule) carry perception risk. For later sets: the staircase supports the operating forecasts; it does not by itself support a re-rating while rates and frontier sentiment stay adverse.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–66

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the first and second sets are highly congruent on everything that drives revenue, EBITDA, recurring free cash flow and capital allocation, and they jar in exactly one region, the lines between operating profit and earnings per share, where the second set's finance inputs are inconsistent with the company's own H1 2026 accounts and both sets mislocate the 2027 to 2029 renewal exposure; the congruence is therefore strong where the case is strong and weak where the case is weak, which is itself the most useful pattern in the work.

Where the sets support and align with each other (the operating spine). Six judgements recur in both sets in the same direction and with compatible magnitudes, and the new AlphaSense evidence confirms each: (1) demand is front-loaded by operators' 2026 capex, additions peak in 2026 and revert toward 2,000 to 2,500 a year from FY2028 (the first set's C-01 path; the second set's 4,100, 3,000, 2,400 and 2,300; Airtel Africa's H1 FY2027 capex of 657 USDm against a held 1.1 USDbn full-year guide implies about 220 USDm a quarter in its second half, a third below the first-half rate); (2) 2026's additions earn a full year in 2027 so reported growth peaks in H1 2027 (first set C-06; second set P-05 and Part 3 of the inputs); (3) margins rise about a point a year on fixed site costs (C-47, C-53; P-01); (4) guidance is conservative and habitually raised (C-13, C-70, C-71; P-06); (5) capex follows demand and is the fast cash lever (C-77; P-03); (6) distributions scale as leverage falls through EBITDA growth rather than debt repayment, and the old 3.0x end-2026 marker will be missed at about 3.3x (C-52, C-19; P-02). The model's regional split (Central and Southern Africa growing fastest, led by the DRC at 2.83x) is consistent with the H1 2026 market table (file 12).

Where the first and second sets contradict each other, and how I resolve each.

  • The 2027 convertible. The first set put the refinancing at about +7 USDm a year of interest; the second at about +2 USDm in the income statement and +8 USDm in cash. File 9 settles it: the bond is a compound instrument under IAS 32, its liability component (148 USDm of the 180 USDm principal at the original allocation) is carried at amortised cost, and the brokers (Jefferies at an 8 percent refinancing rate, Deutsche Bank) model it as debt. The second set is right on the income statement; the first set's number is the cash effect.
  • The share count. The first set called the denominator upside "modest"; the second set put it at about 7 percent of EPS by FY2028 and attributed it to brokers keeping the convertible's 61.5m shares. File 9 shows Jefferies and Deutsche Bank already exclude conversion and hold shares flat. The magnitude survives (consensus net income over consensus EPS still implies about 1.08bn to 1.11bn shares against the second set's 1,001m diluted average for FY2028) but the source is the buyback that brokers have not yet put through their models, not the bond. The practical difference matters for the fourth set: redemption in March 2027 will trigger no consensus change, because it is already assumed; the buyback enters consensus only as brokers update, and the second set's FY2027 "+12 percent EPS above consensus" shrinks once the finance line is corrected (below).
  • The tax rate. The first set called consensus's falling rate "the least credible line"; the second set called the decline mechanically plausible because the dollar charge is driven by operating-company profits while pre-tax profit grows faster. File 10 supports the second set's mechanism (the FY2025 charge of 96.6 USDm contained a 64.0 USDm unwinding of DRC deferred tax assets; unrelievable holding-company losses of 196.3 USDm in Mauritius and 51.6 USDm in the UK make the rate structurally above statutory; management guides cash tax at 5 to 6 percent of revenue) but also the first set's caution (Oman's 15 percent domestic minimum top-up tax from 2025, Congo Brazzaville's rate rise to 30 percent, Mauritius's rise to 17 percent, the lumpy H1-weighted timing, and the interim method that strikes H1 tax at a forecast full-year rate of about 45.6 percent in H1 2026, 18.2 USDm on 39.9 USDm of pre-tax profit). I side with the second set on the direction and keep its dollar tax charges, and with the first set on the level in FY2026.
  • Where the risk sits in time. The first set weighted perception risks over six months and renewal risk over 18 to 36 months; the second set weighted the FY2026 EPS miss and the H2 2027 deceleration. These are compatible once sequenced: 5 November 2026 (sequential fall in additions), March 2027 (FY2026 EPS print), H2 2027 (reported growth slows), 2028 to 2029 (DRC renewals).

Where the second set is internally inconsistent or contradicted by the new evidence (the region that jars).

  • Net financial items. The second set built FY2026 net finance costs bottom-up at about 162 USDm before currency (cash interest about 128, lease interest about 23, accretion about 6.5, fees about 4, less interest income) and 184 USDm including the 21.8 USDm H1 currency loss. But the company reported H1 2026 finance costs of 125.8 USDm (file 7, citing the interim report): with reported operating profit of 162.9 USDm and pre-tax profit of 39.9 USDm (profit 21.7 plus tax 18.2), net finance was about 123 USDm in the half, about 101 USDm before the currency loss. The second set's input leaves only about 61 USDm for H2 2026, impossible against about 64 USDm of half-year cash coupons alone. Lease interest alone was 14.5 USDm in H1 2026 (file 14), about 29 USDm a year against the 23 assumed. H1 2025 tells the same story: a profit of 30.9 USDm after an 18.9 USDm currency gain (file 13) implies underlying net finance near 100 USDm a half, consistent with FactSet's FY2025 net interest of 182 USDm. Even allowing 8 to 12 USDm of one-off refinancing costs in H1 2026 (the April 2026 replacement of the 2028 term loan would have written off unamortised fees), the recurring run-rate is about 180 USDm a year before currency, not 162 to 169. I raise net financial items to about 205 USDm in FY2026 (the H1 actual plus a clean second half of about 82 USDm) and to 180, 181, 177 and 174 USDm in FY2027 to FY2030 (FY2025's 182 less about 6 USDm from the 2026 refinancings, plus about 2 USDm for the convertible's refinancing and about 4 USDm for higher net debt, with the 850 USDm 7.5 percent notes refinanced more cheaply from 2029). Consensus's implied finance charge of 173, 184 and 182 USDm (FY2026 to FY2028) was closer to right than the second set's. Because the debt sits in holding companies whose losses cannot be relieved, a higher finance charge does not reduce the tax charge in dollars; I therefore restate the tax rate from FY2027 so that the second set's dollar tax charges (81, 90, 99 and 109 USDm plus minorities) are preserved on the lower pre-tax profit, giving 37.8, 35.5, 34.2 and 32.7 percent (FY2027 to FY2030) against 36.1, 34.3, 33.2 and 32.1. For FY2026 the H1 charge was struck at management's own forecast full-year rate, so I keep 48.4 percent including minorities. Net income falls to about 72, 142, 174, 204 and 240 USDm.
  • Lease payments. The second set entered lease principal of 41 to 49 USDm a year, below the company's actual lease cash payments of 47.7 USDm in FY2024 and 46.2 USDm in FY2025 and the H1 2026 run-rate of 26.3 USDm (file 14, which also gives lease liabilities flat at 235 USDm and lease interest of 14.5 USDm in the half, so these payments are principal). I raise the line to 52, 54, 56, 58 and 60 USDm. With lease interest, total lease cash is about 80 USDm a year, not 64 to 76.
  • The renewal cohorts. Both sets placed the DRC, Tanzania and Oman cohorts in 2027 to 2028. The company's own undiscounted contracted-revenue schedule (file 11, citing the H1 2026 interim report) shows Middle East and North Africa contracted revenue flat at 64.2 USDm a year from 2027 to 2030: the Omantel contracts signed at the December 2022 acquisition run beyond the window. Central and Southern Africa contracted revenue falls from 446.3 USDm in 2027 to 378.1 USDm in 2029 (-68 USDm, about 15 percent) as Airtel Africa and Orange agreements in the DRC reach their initial terms; East and West Africa falls only from 317.6 to 308.2 USDm. The renewal test of the next three years is therefore concentrated in one market, the DRC, with two counterparties, in 2028 to 2029. This is more concentrated than the earlier sets assumed, but the market is dollarised (the Inwit mechanism of indexation outrunning local-currency revenue is weak), Helios holds about 75 percent of the DRC's independent towers at a 2.83x ratio, and the precedents nearest in kind (American Tower's 12-year renewal with Airtel Africa across about 7,100 sites in FY2025, "cash flow neutral to positive" per Deutsche Bank; IHS and MTN Zambia and Rwanda renewed for ten years in 2024) say "trade, not cliff". The second set's modal concession of about 1 percent of group revenue by FY2029 stands; I would now date it 2028 to 2029 and locate it in the DRC.
  • The currency line. The second set assumed zero after H1 2026 and described an expected loss of 10 to 20 USDm a year. File 13 adds that the group designated certain dollar intercompany loans as net investments in its overseas subsidiaries in 2025, so their retranslation goes to other comprehensive income, and that the disclosed sensitivity of remaining monetary items is small (a 10 percent move in the Tanzanian shilling is about 1.2 USDm). The H1 2026 loss of 21.8 USDm shows the designation is partial. I keep zero in the base; the tail (an FY2023-sized 86 USDm) is less likely than the second set implied but a 20 to 40 USDm year remains plausible.

What the pattern tells the Consumer. Every contradiction between the sets, and every inconsistency within the second set, lies below operating profit. The operating model is congruent across four Analysts, three AlphaSense template reports and the company's own disclosure; the EPS bridge is where each set guessed differently because the company guides nothing there and the brokers model it loosely (consensus FY2026 EPS ranges from 0.054 to 0.100 USD across four houses). The memo should present the EBITDA and cash forecasts as a consensus of the work and the EPS forecasts as the Analyst's own, with the corrections above.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

D3822nd of 30

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: the sell-side narrative articulates the operating dynamics comprehensively and in the same terms as our assessment (front-loaded operator capex, the 2026 carry into 2027 and the slowing after, the fall in capex and the cash harvest, the 175 USDm buyback and the dividend, the pre-IPO exit, the satellite question answered, the leverage advantage over Europe), articulates the below-EBITDA dynamics thinly and inconsistently (the tax rate, the currency line, the finance charge and the EPS denominator are each discussed by one or two houses and modelled by none in a way that matches the accounts), and does not articulate three things at all (the DRC's cash rule, Esengo Towers, the size of the FY2026 EPS miss); the renewals are asked about but not modelled; and there is no investor commentary on the company outside the sell-side that the research could find, so the narrative is four brokers deep and no wider, which makes it both uncrowded and fragile.

Investor commentary. The narrative report searched for investor (non-broker) commentary and found none it could cite: no Twitter, forum or buy-side letter commentary on the company surfaced. The visible non-broker voices are the trade press (ICT Monitor, Telecoms.com, Bankable), a Financial Times and Investors Chronicle note on the H1 results, and Alliance News on the May 2026 price jump. For a FTSE 250 company with a 2,867 USDm market value and 9 to 11 USDm of daily trading, this is thin; the register is institutional and quiet (the sponsors' exit transferred stock to institutions without a visible new narrative holder). The consequences cut both ways: the positive dynamics are not crowded beyond the four Buy ratings (the price did not rise on the July beat-and-raise, which says the marginal holder is not reading the sell-side), and there is no retail or thematic bid to absorb a negative print.

How well known, in sum, for the next 18 months. Fully known (and in the price in the sense that every house has it): the operating staircase, the cash harvest, the distributions, the satellite answer, the overhang's end. Partly known: the depth of the normalisation, the renewals (as a question), the tax and currency drags (as a residual), the denominator (divergently). Unknown: the timing of the growth peak and the first reported deceleration (H1 and H2 2027), the DRC concentration of the renewal and the new builders, Esengo Towers, the DRC cash rule, and the size of the March 2027 EPS miss. For 18 months to three years out: the refresh of the IMPACT 2030 targets is anticipated by all four houses (Jefferies models above the targets), so a raise would confirm rather than surprise; the DRC renewal outcome and the post-2028 distribution step-up are the two events whose outcome is open and whose direction the narrative has not committed to.

How our assessment differs from the narrative, and whether the gap favours the case. Where we are more positive than the narrative and the narrative is silent, the gap is favourable: the share-count effect of the buyback on FY2027 to FY2029 EPS, the resilience of EBITDA through the normalisation (contracted carry), the probability of early delivery of the 2030 targets. Where we are more negative and the narrative is silent, the gap is unfavourable: the FY2026 EPS miss, the finance charge, the DRC concentration of the renewal and the new builders, the capex staying higher in FY2027. The unfavourable gaps mature first (5 November 2026 and March 2027); the favourable ones mature through 2027 and 2028. For the memo's purpose, that sequencing matters more than the net: the narrative will absorb a below-the-line disappointment before it can reward the cash and per-share story.

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 30 October 2026, 01:51 to 02:10 UK, Opus, richie login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Helios Towers (HTWS-GB): answers to the first set of questions, as at 29 October 2026

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reading notes for the Consumer (conventions, checks and data traps)

  • Units. Money is in USD millions unless stated. The share is quoted in pence in London: 205.2p on 29 October 2026, which FactSet's snapshot carries as about 2.73 to 2.77 USD a share; market capitalisation about 2,867 USDm on 1,035m diluted shares; FactSet enterprise value 4,498 USDm. Fiscal year to December; half-year reporting with quarterly trading updates. The next print is the Q3 2026 trading update on 5 November 2026; FY2026 results and FY2027 guidance follow in March 2027.
  • The caution about the latest Earnings Review is cleared. It covers the H1/Q2 2026 results published on 30 July 2026, the same period (June 2026) as the last period in the FactSet block. The file labelled as the earlier Earnings Review ("2025, quarter 4") in fact reviews the Q1 2026 trading update of 7 May 2026; I read it as such.
  • Four EPS bases exist. Consensus is on the brokers' adjusted basis (FY2025 actual 0.033 USD); reported basic EPS was 0.037 USD in FY2025 and 0.019 USD in H1 2026. Never compute growth from reported history to consensus. Consensus EPS divides consensus net income by roughly 1.10bn shares (99.5 USDm over 0.090 USD in FY2026), against about 1,035m to 1,045m shares in issue; I infer below that brokers count the shares underlying the 2027 convertible bond. This matters for the EPS bridge.
  • Leverage has two definitions. The company's net leverage is 3.4x at H1 2026; FactSet's net debt including leases is 1,828 USDm, 3.69x trailing EBITDA (the peer table's 3.7x). Absolute net debt has not yet fallen (1,774 USDm at FY2025, 1,828 USDm at H1 2026); the ratio falls because EBITDA grows.
  • The peer file INH-DE is INDUS Holding AG, a German industrial holding company, not Indus Towers. It is not a peer. I use it only as an analogy (a windfall that flatters reported numbers, a "false friend"). The usable listed comparators in the files are Cellnex, Inwit and Rai Way (European landlords with different economics); the real economic peers, IHS Holding, American Tower's Africa business, SBA and Indus Towers, appear only inside Helios's own research.
  • Figures that conflict between sources are flagged where they matter (for example, independents own about 52 percent of African towers in one report while operators own about 70 percent of African and Middle East towers in another; tenancy guidance histories differ in wording).
  • Outside the files. Where I add public facts not in the files (for example the coupon of the 2027 convertible bond, or the American Tower/Vodafone Idea and IHS/naira precedents), I say so.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Helios Towers plc (HTWS-GB), an independent owner and operator of passive telecommunications tower infrastructure (towers, secure sites and power systems) leasing space to mobile network operators in nine markets (Tanzania, the Democratic Republic of Congo, Oman, Senegal, Malawi, Madagascar, Congo Brazzaville, Ghana, South Africa), reported as one tower-leasing segment in three regions (Central and Southern Africa, East and West Africa, Middle East and North Africa)

Request: T-P1

Peers: IHS Holding (IHS-US; the largest African tower company, diesel-heavy cost base, being acquired by MTN Group); American Tower (AMT-US; its Africa and Asia-Pacific property segment discloses margins and cost actions in comparable markets); SBA Communications (SBAC-US; builds towers in Tanzania and buys ground leases); Indus Towers (INDUSTOWER-IN; large emerging-market tower company with disclosed diesel, solar and smart-metering cost programmes, now entering Africa); Cellnex (CLNX-ES; the Celland ground-lease buyout programme and cost-efficiency plan); Inwit (INW-IT; ground-lease buyouts and lease-cost indexation); private: TowerCo of Africa and Eastcastle Infrastructure (Helios's rivals in the DRC), Esengo Towers (the Vodacom and Orange joint venture licensed in the DRC in April 2026) Inputs: What is the split of fixed costs (ground rent, security, maintenance, staff, central costs) and variable costs (diesel, grid electricity) at Helios Towers and at African tower companies, and how much of the power cost is recovered through power escalators, with what lag? How did Helios's cost of sales and Adjusted EBITDA margin move with tenancy growth and with fuel-price swings from 2021 to 2026, given the company reported FY2025 revenue up 7.8 percent against cost of sales up 1.3 percent (annual report, March 2026)? What is the incremental EBITDA flow-through on a colocation (the company cites about 80 percent) against a new build-to-suit site, and how does group flow-through change when the mix of additions shifts toward new builds, as Morgan Stanley expected for FY2026 (about 500 new towers, May 2026)? How much did higher diesel prices dilute margin percentages at Helios, IHS and Indus Towers in 2022 and in 2026, and over how many quarters did escalators catch up? What has the Project 100 hybrid and solar programme delivered in diesel savings and EBITDA against what was promised, compared with IHS's hybrid rollout and Indus Towers' diesel reduction? Are ground-lease buyouts feasible in Helios's markets given land tenure, and what did Cellnex and Inwit achieve in margin per euro spent? Which costs did IHS and American Tower actually cut during the 2023 to 2024 Nigerian and other emerging-market devaluations, and which proved fixed? How quickly can discretionary capex be cut if operators slow their rollouts, given Helios guided FY2026 discretionary capex of 215 to 245 USDm (July 2026) and IHS cut capex to 246.4 USDm in FY2025 to preserve cash? How do peers' depreciation lives compare with Helios's 2024 extension of tower lives from 15 to 30 years? Focus: How much of each additional dollar of tenancy revenue reaches EBITDA and cash as the mix of additions moves between colocations and new builds and while fuel prices are high, and how flexible site costs and growth capex are if operator spending slows after the 2026 front-loading.

Request: T-P2

Peers: IHS Holding (IHS-US; renegotiated contracts with MTN in Nigeria and changed currency linkage after naira devaluations); American Tower (AMT-US; its Indian tenant Vodafone Idea's payment crisis of 2022 to 2023 and its African master lease agreements); Indus Towers (INDUSTOWER-IN; tenant distress and renewal terms in India); Cellnex (CLNX-ES; the MasOrange contract extension and capped escalators); Inwit (INW-IT; anchor tenants' termination notices in 2026 over uncapped CPI-linked fees); SBA Communications (SBAC-US; Tanzania); private: TowerCo of Africa, Eastcastle Infrastructure, Oman Tower Company Operating conditions: contract renewals at the end of initial ten-to-fifteen-year terms with large multinational operators, in particular in the DRC, Tanzania and Oman; high inflation and currency devaluation in non-dollarised markets (Ghana, Malawi, Tanzania, Madagascar) and de-dollarisation measures in dollarised ones (the Banque Centrale du Congo's ban on physical foreign-currency cash transactions from 9 April 2027); diesel price spikes with Brent above 100 USD; operator consolidation, re-integration of towers and joint building (MTN's acquisition of IHS in 2026, the Vodacom and Orange Esengo Towers joint venture in the DRC, Bharti Airtel-anchored Indus Towers entering Africa); customer payment disputes and liquidity stress; a strong operator capex cycle (Airtel Africa cash capex of 389 USDm in the quarter to June 2026) Inputs: Has Helios Towers renewed any master lease agreement at the end of its initial term, and on what price and term? What price did IHS, American Tower and Indus Towers give back at renewals in Africa and India, and in exchange for what term extension? Did Helios's uncapped CPI escalators and hard-currency pegging clauses hold in full through Ghana's December 2022 default and the cedi and kwacha devaluations, or were concessions, deferrals or caps agreed? What do Airtel Africa, Vodacom, Orange, Axian Telecom and Omantel say publicly about tower costs, renewals, own-tower plans, joint builds and active sharing? How enforceable are all-or-nothing renewal clauses and remaining-value cancellation payments in the DRC, Tanzania and Oman? What explains the rise in Helios's unrecognised potential contract revenue from 18.8 USDm to 46.8 USDm in FY2025, flagged by the auditor as a key audit matter, and is it linked to power-escalator disputes? How are amendment fees and secondary-tenant pricing set, and have they held? What does the Inwit dispute of 2026 show about how operators respond when indexed fees outrun their own revenue per user, and does the same mechanism apply in Helios's markets? Test the expert claims in the research that African tower lease rates fall 20 to 30 percent at renewal once initial capital is amortised, against the counter-claim that dollar and CPI linkage are red lines tower companies have held. Focus: How much of Helios's lease pricing, including uncapped indexation, survives the 2027 to 2028 renewal cohorts and high-inflation conditions, and whether customers' new tower options change the renewal outcome.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: The investment case depends on whether the 2026 surge in tenancy additions and the start of cash returns are self-reinforcing, or are balanced by customer pushback on cost and by a payback from operators' front-loaded capex. Peers: IHS Holding (IHS-US; low tenancy ratio, retrenchment and sale to an operator); American Tower (AMT-US; long-run lease-up and buyback loops in mature markets, and churn when tenants consolidate); Indus Towers (INDUSTOWER-IN; operator-anchored tower company and tenant-distress cycles in India); Cellnex (CLNX-ES; deleveraging into distributions); Inwit (INW-IT; anchor tenants' cost revolt); SBA Communications (SBAC-US); private: Esengo Towers (operator joint build in the DRC), TowerCo of Africa Inputs: Density loop: each colocation raises a site's return and lowers cost per tenant, which wins the next colocation; how fast have tenancy ratios risen in mature and newer African markets (Helios reported the DRC at 2.83x, Tanzania 2.73x, Senegal 1.21x and Madagascar 1.32x at H1 2026) and what stopped the loop elsewhere? Cash-return loop: falling leverage releases buybacks and dividends, which lift per-share cash flow, credit ratings and the cost of capital; what is the evidence at Helios (Moody's upgrade to Ba3, February 2026; buyback raised to 175 USDm, September 2026) and at peers? Customer-cost balancing loop: more tenants, uncapped CPI and power escalators raise operators' tower bills, which pushes operators toward joint builds, active sharing, re-integration (MTN and IHS) or captive tower companies (Indus Towers), which caps future tenancy growth and pricing. Front-loading balancing loop: operators pull capex forward (Airtel Africa's "deliberate frontloading", July 2026), which is followed by lower additions; how long did comparable operator capex surges last in India, Africa and the US, and what followed? Capex-funding balancing loop: higher growth capex to meet demand lowers free cash flow and slows deleveraging and distributions. Valuation loop: satellite direct-to-cell headlines depress tower multiples regardless of fundamentals. Focus: Whether 2026's record tenancy additions strengthen or weaken the conditions for future additions and pricing, and which loop dominates over 2027 to 2029.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Earnings per share is a thin residual of EBITDA after depreciation, interest, currency movements and tax, and sell-side EPS forecasts have been cut repeatedly while EBITDA forecasts rose, so the bridge inputs decide whether the forecasts are met. Peers: Airtel Africa (AAF-GB; reports tax, withholding and currency effects in the same countries); Vodacom Group (VOD-ZA; DRC and Tanzania tax and repatriation); IHS Holding (IHS-US; currency losses on intercompany and hard-currency debt, effective tax rate in Africa); American Tower (AMT-US; international tax structure and interest); Cellnex (CLNX-ES) and Inwit (INW-IT; financing costs and refinancing of tower debt) Inputs: the effective tax rate (reported at 38.9 percent in FY2024 and 71.0 percent in FY2025 on FactSet data; tax expense 96.6 USDm against cash tax of 45.5 USDm in FY2025, annual report), its drivers (minimum taxes in loss-making entities such as the DRC, withholding taxes on upstreaming to Mauritius, non-deductible holding-company interest) and the structural rate; finance costs (163.7 USDm in FY2025), the April 2026 issue of 500 USDm of 6.75 percent notes due 2031, the coupon and remaining amount of the convertible bonds due 2027 after the 120 USDm tender, the 250 USDm term loan raised in May 2026, interest-rate swaps and lease interest; non-cash currency movements on intercompany loans (86 USDm in FY2023; the main cause of the fall in H1 2026 profit to 21.7 USDm from 30.9 USDm) and how brokers treat them; depreciation after the 2024 extension of tower lives from 15 to 30 years and its path as capex rises; the share count (1,045,234,504 shares at 30 April 2026), the 175 USDm buyback to December 2027, dilution from the convertible and from share plans (including the proposal to remove the 5 percent discretionary share-plan cap), and the share count brokers use; minority interests, including in Oman; open tax assessments in the DRC, Tanzania and Congo Brazzaville and the unrecognised contract revenue as possible one-off items Focus: Which below-EBITDA lines decide whether brokers' FY2026 to FY2028 EPS forecasts are met, in particular the structural tax rate and the finance charge after the 2027 convertible is refinanced, and what share count is the right denominator.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 31 October 2026, 02:11 to 02:27 UK, Opus, richie login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Helios Towers (HTWS-GB): answers to the second set of questions (financial forecasts), as at 31 October 2026

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reading notes for the Consumer

  • Units and bases. USD millions unless stated; fiscal year to December; half-year reporting with quarterly trading updates. "EBITDA" means the company's Adjusted EBITDA, which is also the brokers' basis (FY2025: 471.1 USDm; FactSet's operating EBITDA is 464, about 0.9 points of margin lower, because it deducts share-based payments and similar items). The model's history rows use FactSet's operating line; compare my FY2026 margin of 56.0 percent with 55.2 percent (FY2025 Adjusted), not with 54.3 percent.
  • My base case in one line. Tenancy additions of about 4,100 (FY2026), 3,000 (FY2027), 2,400 (FY2028) and 2,300 a year after; revenue 948, 1,043, 1,114, 1,175 and 1,241 USDm (FY2026 to FY2030); EBITDA 531, 598, 649, 693 and 743 USDm; net income 83, 152, 184, 213 and 248 USDm; free cash flow after leases and all capex -30, +102, +171, +216 and +243 USDm. The full table is in P-10 and in the forecast-inputs form.
  • The most important new finding for the Consumer: FactSet's "brokers' basis" actual EPS appears to be reported net income divided by diluted shares, not an adjusted figure. FY2025: reported net income 39.2 USDm over 1,180m weighted diluted shares = 0.0332 USD, which is the 0.033 USD FactSet records as the brokers'-basis actual; FY2024: 33.5 USDm over about 1,117m diluted shares = 0.030 USD, again the recorded actual. Consequence: non-cash currency losses on intercompany loans, which brokers do not forecast, will count against consensus when actuals print. The 21.8 USDm loss in H1 2026 is already in FY2026.
  • Where I differ from the first set (each argued in the relevant answer): (1) refinancing the 2027 convertible is close to neutral for the P&L, not +7 USDm, because IFRS already charges the bond at about 6.8 percent; (2) the falling consensus tax rate from FY2027 is mechanically plausible, and the real tax risk is concentrated in FY2026 and in one-off swings; (3) the share-count upside to consensus is larger than "modest": consensus divides by about 1.08bn to 1.11bn shares even in FY2028 and FY2029, ignoring the buyback and the convertible's March 2027 maturity; (4) FY2026 consensus EPS (0.090 USD) is the single most likely miss in the case, by about 15 to 20 percent, while FY2027 and FY2028 consensus EPS are more likely beaten than missed.
  • Estimates flagged as mine. Lease payments, the split of finance costs, the H1 2026 tax charge and the coupon of the 850 USDm 2029 notes (7.5 percent, outside the files, from public filings) are not given directly in the files; I state where I infer them.

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Segments: revenue and margin by segment, where the company reports them

The company reports one segment (tower leasing) in three regions; it does not disclose regional EBITDA in the files except Middle East and North Africa (Oman) at about 72 percent in 2024, and it restated segment EBITDA for corporate recharges in Q1 2026. My regional revenue (USDm):

Region FY2025 actual FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030
Central and Southern Africa (the DRC, Congo Brazzaville, Malawi, Madagascar, South Africa) 431.4 483 (+12.0%) 534 (+10.5%) 571 (+7.0%) 603 (+5.5%) 637 (+5.6%)
East and West Africa (Tanzania, Senegal, Ghana) 348.2 381 (+9.5%) 418 (+9.5%) 445 (+6.6%) 470 (+5.5%) 496 (+5.6%)
Middle East and North Africa (Oman) 74.5 83 (+12.0%) 91 (+9.0%) 97 (+6.5%) 102 (+5.5%) 108 (+5.6%)
Group 854.1 948 1,043 1,114 1,175 1,241

Central and Southern Africa grows fastest in FY2026 and FY2027 because the DRC (tenancy ratio 2.83x) led the 2026 lease-up; from FY2028 it carries the most exposure to Esengo, Eastcastle and TowerCo of Africa in the rural DRC, and to the DRC renewal cohort. Oman's 2026 growth reflects Omantel's 5G rollout and 3G retirement (454 tenancy additions in H1 2026) and Vodafone Oman's densification; its margin (about 70 to 72 percent) is above the group because it is grid-powered. FactSet's regional consensus (mean, FY2026 to FY2028) is Central and Southern Africa 465, 515 and 562 USDm; East and West Africa 380, 413 and 442 USDm; Middle East and North Africa 81, 87 and 94 USDm (it sums to less than the group consensus because fewer brokers contribute).

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Tenancy additions (the swing input): about 4,100 in FY2026 (Q1 1,406 and Q2 1,105 actual; about 850 in Q3 and 740 in Q4, above the 495 to 745 a quarter implied by guidance, on the company's record of beating and a pipeline into FY2027), 3,000 in FY2027, 2,400 in FY2028 and 2,300 a year in FY2029 and FY2030 (management's medium-term "about 6 percent a year"). The FY2027 and FY2028 fall reflects operators' front-loading (Airtel Africa's own guidance implies its quarterly capex falls by about 40 percent from the June 2026 quarter). Each 1,000 additions moves annualised EBITDA by about 20 USDm.
  • Tenancy ratio from 2.26x (H1 2026) to about 2.32x (end-2026), 2.53x (end-2028) and 2.69x (end-2030), on about 400 to 500 new towers a year.
  • Revenue per average tenancy roughly flat at about 27.6k to 27.8k USD: CPI escalators (about +2 points a year on lease revenue, uncapped, US CPI on dollar revenue) offset by lower revenue on incremental tenancies (amendments are 13 percent of tenancies; newer markets) and currency translation (about -0.3 to -1.2 points). Power escalators add about 1 point in FY2026 at nil EBITDA (Brent near 111 USD) and are assumed neutral thereafter.
  • Renewals: a modest concession on the 2027 to 2028 DRC, Tanzania and Oman cohorts (escalator caps or freezes in exchange for ten-year extensions) worth about -0.5 points of revenue in each of FY2028 and FY2029, about 11 USDm of revenue and EBITDA by FY2029. A material concession is a downside scenario, not the base.
  • Margin: incremental EBITDA margin 64 percent (FY2026; power pass-through and new builds), then 70 to 76 percent; margin bridge FY2025 to FY2030 +4.7 points: lease-up on fixed site costs about +3.0, overhead leverage about +1.5, power efficiency retained by Helios about +0.7, new-build dilution, pass-through and the renewal concession about -0.5.
  • Depreciation: core depreciation and amortisation 168, 178, 186, 193 and 199 USDm (consensus 168, 180, 184), rising with the 2026 capex surge (towers depreciated over up to 30 years since 2024; power equipment over much shorter lives); plus 18, 12, 12, 13 and 13 USDm of other non-cash operating items.
  • Finance: about 162 USDm before currency in FY2026 (cash interest about 128, lease interest about 23, convertible accretion about 6.5, fees about 4, less interest income), then 169, 171, 168 and 167 USDm; the convertible's refinancing adds about 2 USDm to the P&L (it already bears an effective rate of about 6.8 percent) and about 8 USDm to cash interest; the 850 USDm 7.5 percent 2029 notes (coupon outside the files) are assumed refinanced more cheaply from FY2029 on the Ba3 and BB- ratings.
  • Tax: cash tax rising from 9.7 percent of EBITDA (FY2025) to about 13 percent by FY2030 (56, 69, 78, 87, 97 USDm) plus about 12 to 18 USDm a year of deferred tax; the rate on pre-tax profit falls because pre-tax profit grows much faster than the tax base.
  • Capital allocation: dividends 25 USDm (FY2026) growing just over 10 percent a year; buybacks 52, 99, 110, 150, 180 USDm; no acquisitions; leverage (net debt including leases over EBITDA, FactSet basis) 3.52x, 3.18x, 2.89x, 2.66x and 2.46x at year-ends; about 3.3x on the company's basis at end-2026.

My FY2027 figures (revenue about 1,043 USDm, EBITDA about 598 USDm) sit at or slightly above consensus (1,046 and 594 USDm); I expect FY2027 guidance in March 2027 to open near 585 to 600 USDm.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • Revenue: company, FactSet and brokers' bases are the same (FY2025 854.1 USDm).
  • EBITDA: consensus uses the company's Adjusted EBITDA (FY2025 471.1 USDm, the "brokers' basis" actual 471); FactSet's operating EBITDA, which fills the model's history, is lower (464 USDm, 54.3 percent) because it deducts share-based payments and similar items; in H1 2026 the gap was 7 USDm (257.0 against 250). My margins are on the Adjusted basis; to compare with the model's history, subtract about 0.8 to 1.3 points.
  • Operating profit: statutory operating profit is Adjusted EBITDA less depreciation and amortisation less other adjusting items (H1 2026: 257.0 less about 80 less about 14 gives 162.9 USDm reported). Consensus EBIT (337, 402, 446 USDm, three brokers) is about 12 to 23 USDm below consensus EBITDA less consensus depreciation, consistent with brokers deducting these items. My depreciation input includes them.
  • EPS: FactSet's "brokers' basis" actuals appear to be reported net income over weighted diluted shares: FY2025 39.2 USDm over 1,180m = 0.033 USD (the recorded actual); FY2024 33.5 USDm over about 1,117m = 0.030 USD. Consensus net income over consensus EPS implies about 1.105bn, 1.108bn, 1.076bn and 1.10bn shares (FY2026 to FY2029), so brokers divide by a diluted count that keeps the convertible's about 61.5m potential shares and largely ignores buybacks. My cross-check below is on basic average shares (1,033m, 998m, 966m, 929m, 885m) to match the model's mechanics; on a diluted basis comparable with consensus (adding about 35m share-plan shares, and the convertible until March 2027) my EPS is 0.073, 0.146, 0.184, 0.221 and 0.269 USD against consensus 0.090, 0.130, 0.170 and 0.200 USD (FY2026 to FY2029).
  • Currency on intercompany loans: included in reported (and, on the evidence above, brokers'-basis) actuals but forecast by no one; FY2026 already carries -21.8 USDm from H1.
  • Free cash flow: the company's recurring free cash flow is before discretionary capex and working capital (my 227, 265, 300, 334, 372 USDm); FactSet's free cash flow is operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex, before intangibles and leases (FY2025 90.6 USDm); FactSet's consensus free cash flow line (226, 202, 242, 509 USDm) mixes definitions and should not be read as a series. My cross-check is after all capex and lease payments, before acquisitions, dividends and buybacks.

Layer 3 and Layer 4: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months / Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

Written 2 November 2026, 10:52 to 11:13 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Helios Towers (HTWS-GB): answers to the third and fourth sets of questions, as at 2 November 2026

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reading notes for the Consumer

  • Units, bases and dates. USD millions unless stated; the share is quoted in pence in London (205.2p on 29 October 2026, about 2.73 to 2.77 USD; market value about 2,867 USDm; FactSet enterprise value 4,498 USDm; net debt including leases 1,828 USDm at June 2026). "EBITDA" is the company's Adjusted EBITDA, which is the brokers' basis. Consensus is the FactSet median of four brokers (two for FY2029) at 28 October 2026. The Q3 2026 trading update falls on 5 November 2026, three days after this is written; nothing below assumes its content (the AlphaSense report commissioned on it, file 12, confirms it was still pending), and I state dated tests for the Consumer to read against it.
  • The model of record (after model update 1) reproduces the second set's inputs: revenue 948, 1,043, 1,114, 1,175 and 1,241 USDm (FY2026 to FY2030); EBITDA 531, 598, 649, 693 and 743; net income 83, 152, 184, 213 and 247; EPS 0.08, 0.15, 0.19, 0.23 and 0.29 USD; equity free cash flow -84, 80, 149, 193 and 219; a return at the guideline fair multiple (26.28x two-year-forward P/E against 15.7x today) of +127 percent to the December 2027 column and +200 percent to the June 2029 column, almost entirely from earnings growth.
  • The flagged gaps (free cash flow in every year, the second set's cross-check against the model) are differences of basis, not of view; they are explained in full in the section below and do not show an input to be wrong.
  • I change three inputs on other grounds (net financial items in every year, the tax rate from FY2027, lease payments in every year); they are in inputs-changed.md and argued in D-01 and D-04. Their effect, which I use as "my" figures below: net income about 72, 142, 174, 204 and 240 USDm (FY2026 to FY2030) against the model's 83, 152, 184, 213 and 247; basic EPS about 0.070, 0.142, 0.180, 0.220 and 0.272 USD; on the diluted basis comparable with consensus about 0.064, 0.135, 0.174 and 0.211 USD (FY2026 to FY2029) against consensus 0.090, 0.130, 0.170 and 0.200. Revenue, EBITDA, margins, capex and distributions are unchanged.
  • New evidence since the second set. Six AlphaSense reports answering the second set's own questions arrived on 1 and 2 November 2026 (files 9 to 14). The findings that change a judgement: (1) the covering brokers already treat the March 2027 convertible as debt with no dilution, so the share-count upside to consensus comes from the buyback, not from the bond's redemption; (2) the company's own contracted-revenue profile places the 2027 to 2029 renewal exposure in the DRC (Airtel Africa and Orange), not in Oman, whose contracted revenue is flat to 2030; (3) H1 2026 finance costs were 125.8 USDm, a run-rate the second set's finance inputs cannot accommodate; (4) lease cash flows run above the second set's inputs; (5) management guided cash tax at about 5 to 6 percent of revenue to 2030 (capital markets day, November 2025); (6) certain dollar intercompany loans were designated as net investments in 2025, moving part of the currency exposure out of the income statement; (7) the sell-side narrative covers the operating story comprehensively, the below-EBITDA story thinly, and the DRC cash rule and Esengo Towers not at all; no investor (non-broker) commentary on the company could be found at all.

The flagged gaps between the second set's cross-check and the model, explained

The one-page summary flags free cash flow in all five years: the second set's -30, 102, 171, 216 and 243 USDm against the model's -83.7, 79.5, 149.3, 192.5 and 218.9. The second set defined its cross-check as Adjusted EBITDA less cash tax, cash interest, total lease payments, all capex and the change in working capital. The model computes free cash flow from the profit and loss inputs: EBITDA plus net financial items (which, as entered, include non-cash items: the 21.8 USDm H1 2026 currency loss, the convertible's accretion of about 6.5 USDm and amortised fees of about 4 USDm in FY2026, and about 10 USDm a year of accretion-type and fee items thereafter) less tax at the input rate (which includes about 12 to 18 USDm a year of deferred tax and 4 to 8 USDm of minority interests) less capex, working capital and lease principal. The second set predicted the resulting gap in advance: about 54 USDm in FY2026 and 22 to 24 USDm a year thereafter, giving about -84, 79, 149, 193 and 219. The model's figures match that prediction to within 1 USDm in every year. Each year's gap is therefore the non-cash content of two input lines plus minorities, not a disagreement about volumes, margins, capex or distributions. No input is shown to be wrong by the gap, and the EPS cross-check matches in every year (the FY2030 0.28 against 0.29 is rounding).

Two consequences for the Consumer. First, the model's equity free cash flow understates cash generation by about 22 to 24 USDm a year from FY2027 and its year-end net debt (1,984 USDm at end-FY2026 rising to 2,031 at end-FY2027) is correspondingly about 50 to 150 USDm too high by FY2030; the second set's net financial debt path (1,644 to 1,573 USDm excluding leases) is the better cash reading. Second, because the return in the model is computed from EPS at a P/E multiple, the gap has no effect on the return of record. The three input changes I make below do lower both bases of free cash flow, by about 17 USDm a year of cash (lease principal and lease interest) and by more on the model's basis; they also lower EPS, which does affect the return.

Appendix The share-price co-movement table the Investment Analyst was given

Co-movement of HTWS-GB with its dashboard peers (computed 2026-10-30 from the master dashboard's daily prices)

What this is: daily close-to-close returns over rolling windows of 126 trading days (about six months). Correlation: how closely the daily moves line up (1 = always together, 0 = unrelated). Beta: how far the stock moves for a 1% move in the basket. R-squared: the share of the stock's daily price variance the peer basket explains; the remainder is the stock's own (idiosyncratic) movement. Basket: the peers below, equal-weighted. Prices only; the multiple's own history is in the financials block.

Peers (from the dashboard data file): Cellnex (CLNX-ES), Inwit (INW-IT), Rai Way (RWAY-IT)

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for the memo

Research requests for the next set

Company: Helios Towers plc (HTWS-GB): an independent owner and operator of passive telecommunications tower infrastructure (towers, secure sites and power systems) leasing space to mobile network operators in nine markets (Tanzania, the Democratic Republic of Congo, Oman, Senegal, Malawi, Madagascar, Congo Brazzaville, Ghana, South Africa), reported as one tower-leasing segment in three regions (Central and Southern Africa, East and West Africa, Middle East and North Africa).

Request: OWN-1

Question: For Helios Towers plc (HTWS-GB), what did the Q3 2026 trading update of 5 November 2026 report for organic tenancy additions by market, new sites, the group tenancy ratio, quarterly revenue and Adjusted EBITDA, and any change to the FY2026 guidance set on 30 July 2026 (tenancy additions 3,500 to 4,000; Adjusted EBITDA 520 to 535 USDm; recurring free cash flow 220 to 235 USDm; discretionary capex 215 to 245 USDm); what management said about the 2027 order pipeline, 2027 capex, the March 2027 convertible bond, the share buyback and the DRC master lease renewals; how the shares reacted on the day and in the following week; and what each covering house (Jefferies, Deutsche Bank, Morgan Stanley, Peel Hunt, New Street Research) changed in its FY2026 and FY2027 forecasts for revenue, Adjusted EBITDA, EPS and free cash flow, its target price and its rating after the update?

Request: OWN-2

Question: For Helios Towers plc (HTWS-GB), what made up the H1 2026 finance costs of 125.8 USDm reported in the interim results of 30 July 2026 (interest on each debt instrument, lease interest of 14.5 USDm, the convertible bond's accretion, amortisation or write-off of arrangement fees including any on the April 2026 refinancing of the 2028 term loan with 500 USDm of 6.75 percent senior notes due 2031, hyperinflation net monetary items under IAS 29, derivative fair-value movements and the 21.8 USDm non-cash currency loss on intercompany loans) and the finance income against them; what the same breakdown was for FY2025 (finance costs of 163.7 USDm) and H1 2025 (which included a currency gain of 18.9 USDm); what full-year effective tax rate the company applied in the H1 2026 interim accounts and what it said about cash tax for FY2026 and the 5 to 6 percent of revenue guided at the capital markets day of 6 November 2025; and what net finance cost, tax charge, diluted share count and treatment of non-cash currency movements each covering broker (Jefferies, Deutsche Bank, Morgan Stanley, Peel Hunt, New Street Research) uses in its FY2026 and FY2027 EPS forecasts?

Request: OWN-3

Question: For Helios Towers plc (HTWS-GB), which master lease agreements with Airtel Africa and Orange in the Democratic Republic of Congo reach the end of their initial terms in 2028 and 2029 (the H1 2026 interim report's undiscounted contracted revenue for Central and Southern Africa falls from 446.3 USDm in 2027 to 378.1 USDm in 2029), what Helios Towers, Airtel Africa and Orange have said publicly in 2025 and 2026 about renewal timing, pricing, indexation and term for DRC tower leases, what sites and tenants Esengo Towers (the Vodacom and Orange joint venture licensed in April 2026 to invest 179 USDm in 1,000 rural towers over four years) and Eastcastle Infrastructure (which raised a 32.8 USDm loan in August 2026 to grow from 1,072 to 1,800 DRC sites) have built by the end of 2026, and what the Banque Centrale du Congo's ban on physical foreign-currency cash transactions from 9 April 2027 means in practice for a tower company's lease collections, supplier and ground-rent payments and cash upstreaming from the DRC, including anything Helios Towers, Vodacom, Airtel Africa or Orange has said about it?
